Medartis · MED-CH · the new Memo conveyor's card of 16 November 2026

What the Investment Analyst wrote behind each of the eleven ratings: Medartis

The Investment Analyst's answers to the 101 questions and its 13 group judgements, 45,789 words, set out under the eleven ratings: each rating, then each of its lettered sub-items, then the answers beneath.

Word for word. Every answer is the Investment Analyst's text exactly as written; a script checked that every word is on this page, in order. What is changed is layout only: answers are grouped by rating rather than by layer; each answer is titled with the question as it was set (the Investment Analyst's own heading appears beneath only where it adds words); an answer that belongs under more than one sub-item is shown in full once, at its first place, and as a one-line link everywhere else.

Which answer sits where comes from the same table the ratings map uses. Each answer carries its layer and group, because "positive" means something different in each layer. Each layer's own material (what it was asked, what its scores mean, the Investment Analyst's notes and appendices) is at the end, so nothing is left out.

The ratings alone: the ratings map.

How to read the scores and letters

The Investment Analyst ended each answer with a score from 1 to 100: 100 is the most positive for an investment case, 1 the most negative, and 50 neither. What "positive" refers to differs by layer, so each layer states its own meaning (listed just below).

Where the Rater has scored this stock (every memo from 3 November 2026), the number in each pill is the Rater's, set after the memo was written from the Investment Analyst's judgements against a key per group of questions; the Investment Analyst's own score is kept in the pill's tooltip. The letter beside each score is read from the one canonical source for ratings, the same file the eleven ratings page and the memo scorecards read; nothing is graded on this page. The letter follows the item's rule: for most items this stock's rank among the stocks scored on the same scale on that same question or group judgement, re-cut each night (Richard's ruling of 4-Nov-26: every letter by rank; in the file's words: A above 90%; B+ above 82.5% to 90%; B above 75% to 82.5%; C+ above 66.67% to 75%; C above 58.33% to 66.67%; C- above 50% to 58.33%; D above 25% to 50%; F up to 25%); on the External change forces and Internal change forces items the number carries a sign (50 = neither positive nor negative) and the letter is the rank, so read the two together. “37th of 56” is the stock's place among them; a dashed outline marks a provisional grade (fewer than 30 stocks scored).

What the scores mean in each layer

Layer 1

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

Layer 2

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the likely effect on the company's financial forecasts is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

Layer 3 and Layer 4

Finish each answer and each judgement on a group or set with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For the third set's questions, the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for the predictability of the company's financial forecasts over the next 18 months, and so for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

For the fourth set's questions, the score says how favourable for an investment case in the company the gap is between our assessment and what current guidance, sell-side estimates and the stock market narrative already reflect: 100 the most favourable, 1 the least, 50 neither.

Inputs

1

External headwinds/tailwinds

–not yet rated

Memo topic 1, : the Rater's score

Behind the rating as a whole

Group judgement

External change forces

Layer 1 · External change forces

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group. The rating above stands for it, so it carries no number of its own.

The external environment is a genuine but narrow tailwind. Medartis sits in the fastest-growing part of orthopaedics, has a regulator- and payer-created new market in TOUCH, and faces a supplier base with no leverage over it. Against that, the three external forces it cannot control (the franc, the discount rate applied to small growth stocks, and the cost of competing against larger rivals who are now responding in hand and wrist) all bite at the level where Medartis is weakest: reported earnings and the multiple. For the model, external forces justify organic growth in the high teens through 2027 with good visibility, but they argue for modelling reported margins below constant-currency margins and for treating 2027 margin expansion as dependent on the tariff roll-off (mechanical, high confidence) and on the franc (unforecastable, unhedged). The net external picture is better for revenue than for EPS.

1a

External headwinds/threats

6 answers
C-0238C-0562C-0738C-0955C-3554C-3966
C-02

To what extent, and in which direction, is competitive intensity changing?

F3828th of 36

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement. Competitive intensity is rising, and specifically in the two places that matter most: US hand and wrist (where Medartis is trying to grow from about 2% share) and European plating (its home base). It has not turned into price aggression; the majors remain rational on price and compete on product breadth, bundling and digital tools. The one area of reduced intensity is thumb-base arthroplasty, where TOUCH has no direct US equivalent today.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • Acumed made three moves within three months: the Acu-Loc NEXT wrist system (first case December 2025), the ExsoMed acquisition in intramedullary hand fixation (September 2025), and TECHFIT in patient-specific CMF (November 2025), plus the ASCent fixed-price programme for surgery centres. Acumed is the incumbent in volar wrist plates (one million cases) and this is a direct response aimed at Medartis's largest franchise.
  • Stryker launched the 33-plate Pangea system in 2024 and completed first European cases in mid-2026, entering Medartis's home market.
  • Globus claims 80%+ portfolio parity after its elbow launch; its trauma segment grew 30.4% in Q1 2026.
  • Zimmer Biomet folded its lower extremities and trauma into Paragon 28's dedicated channel (Paragon 28 grew over 18% in Q3 2026), raising the bar in foot and ankle, where Medartis's growth slowed to 10.4% in H1 2026.
  • In Japan the former distributor became a direct competitor after Medartis went direct; wrist sales fell below the prior year.
  • Offsetting: management notes peer integration disruption (Zimmer/Paragon 28, Enovis/Lima) frees surgeon accounts; Johnson & Johnson plans to separate DePuy Synthes by mid-2027, which may disrupt its sales force for a time.

What it means for the model. Keep plating price flat to slightly negative (see C-45), assume Medartis keeps winning US share but with rising sales and training cost per incremental surgeon, and stress-test lower extremities and Japan for slower growth. The main competitive risk to the model is not price; it is the cost of winning.

C-05

How are input costs, input availability and supplier concentration changing, and is the net effect a headwind or a tailwind?

A623rd of 36

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement. Physical inputs are a non-issue: titanium is commoditised, sourced from low-risk jurisdictions and from suppliers within 200 km of the plants. The input costs that matter are three non-material ones: the US tariff (falling, a tailwind from 2027), the Swiss franc cost of the Basel labour base (a structural headwind, partly mitigated by Warsaw), and the third-party NX nail supply agreement expiring in 2027 (a transition risk). Net: a modest tailwind over 18 months.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • Over 90% of suppliers sit within 200 km of Basel and Warsaw and account for more than 95% of related procurement; nine audits in 2025 found no critical deviations; 32 tonnes of titanium a year, up to 35% recycled feedstock; virgin titanium from the US and Japan. Polymer prices rose 32% and 27% year on year in Q2 and Q3 2026 (Straumann source), immaterial for a titanium implant maker.
  • Basel is a high-cost, 24/7 robotic site; the franc cost base is the real input cost (see C-07). Warsaw production of semi-finished plates and screws converts part of the cost base to dollars, a natural hedge for the 19% of sales in the US.
  • The proprietary Titan Nail (launched August 2026) replaces the third-party NX nail before the agreement expires in 2027; management says the transition will be phased to existing NX volumes. Moving from distributed product to own IP should lift gross margin on that line, but any gap in supply or surgeon conversion would show in lower extremities.
C-07

To what extent is a macro force acting on the company, on its sector and on its industry, and at which level does it bite hardest?

F3825th of 36

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement. The macro force that bites hardest is the Swiss franc, and it bites hardest at company level because Medartis is a Swiss-cost, foreign-revenue business with no hedging at all. The second force is the discount rate, which bites at market and stock level because Medartis trades on a high multiple of near-term earnings; the third is the US consumer and hospital administrator, which bites at industry level (elective orthopaedics) more than at Medartis's trauma-weighted business.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • Franc, company level. FY2025: reported sales growth 21.2% against 25.6% at constant currency (about -4.4 points); core EBITDA margin about -1.2 points from currency; FX losses of CHF 5.2m in the financial result (from a CHF 0.1m gain in 2024), which halved core net profit to CHF 6.1m. H1 2026: a weak dollar cut US reported growth by 11 points; currency cost 0.6 points of gross margin; but the financial result improved by CHF 6.7m on FX gains (on about CHF 200m of intercompany loans). Management's spot-rate guidance for FY2026 is a 90 to 100 basis point margin drag. Sensitivities (annual report): 10% USD/CHF move about CHF 1.9m of pre-tax profit; 10% AUD/CHF about CHF 1.3m; 10% EUR/CHF about CHF 0.4m the other way. Zero derivatives: the policy prioritises cash over accounting smoothness (unlike Stryker, J&J, Integra). Bank forecasts in the Risks report point to further franc strength (Maybank USD/CHF 0.785 by Q1 2027; Morgan Stanley EUR/CHF 0.87 in early 2027). The Geography digest states the structural point: for unhedged Swiss medtech, reported margins are a currency reading first.
  • Discount rate, market level. The Fitness for Fighting digest reads the regime as risk-off beneath an index at its high, with US Treasuries near 5%, Fragility at Double Underweight and Momentum at Underweight. Medartis is small, illiquid (USD 2m to 4m a day) and priced at about 30x two-year-forward earnings: it is exactly the profile that the "small-cap growth cracks first" pattern punishes.
  • US consumer and administrator, industry level. Large-joint slowdown, prior-authorisation denials, capital budgets tightening (only 45% of hospital executives expect higher capital spending, Getinge source). Medartis's plating is insulated; TOUCH is partly exposed.

What it means for the model. Model constant-currency and reported lines separately; put currency in as an explicit, unhedged translation and transaction line (revenue, gross margin, and below-EBIT FX result); expect brokers' reported-currency numbers and the share price to follow the reported figures.

C-09

What other material external dynamic, positive or negative, is at work?

–55

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement. Three further dynamics matter. Positive: orthopaedic industry restructuring (DePuy Synthes separation, Zimmer integrating Paragon 28, Integra cutting orthopaedic lines, Stryker reportedly divesting CMF) loosens surgeon relationships that a focused specialist can win. Positive but uncertain: a healthcare rotation in European equities since mid-October 2026. Negative: the market's treatment of the Swiss and European orthopaedic peer group, which has de-rated sharply on any deceleration.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • Peer M&A: Zimmer bought Paragon 28 for about USD 1.1bn (April 2025); ZimVie went private at a 99% premium (October 2025); Integra discontinued up to 70% of its orthopaedic lines; Leadership digest notes Stryker is divesting its CMF business. Each creates displaced accounts and, separately, an M&A valuation reference for specialists.
  • The Industry digest's relative strength for Healthcare moved to A (13 November 2026), but Medtech consumables reads F; Medartis's own relative strength is at the 88th percentile and the stock is up 45% in twelve months, so it is already an outlier within its sector.
  • Medacta, the closest Swiss orthopaedic peer, is down 27% in twelve months to a P/E at the bottom of its ten-year range after growth slowed to 9.7% at constant currency in H1 2026 from 18.5% in FY2025 (Deutsche Bank: "outgrowing the market, but not expectations"). This is the clearest precedent of how the market treats a Swiss orthopaedic grower whose growth halves.
C-35

To what extent are new entrants (from adjacent markets; standard entrants; orthogonal or innovative entrants) entering or threatening this market, and how much of a threat is each type?

D5420th of 36

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement. The threat from each type is low-to-moderate over four years.

  • Adjacent (large-joint and spine companies extending into extremities: Globus, Zimmer via Paragon 28, Stryker's Wright legacy): the most real threat, already happening, moderate.
  • Standard (low-cost local makers, Korean and Chinese implant makers moving up): low in developed markets (MDR and FDA costs; the Straumann CEO notes challengers can no longer use premium devices as predicates), moderate in LATAM and Asian public tenders, which NeoOrtho addresses.
  • Orthogonal (3D-printing firms, hospital point-of-care printing, AI planning platforms, bioresorbable implants): low near term, rising over five years in complex CMF and reconstruction; Medartis's CADskills and CMX positions mean it is partly one of these entrants itself.
C-39

Where relevant or material, how are suppliers behaving: a support to the company, or a source of risk?

A662nd of 36

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement. Suppliers are a support. Titanium is commoditised with alternative sources, the supply base is local and audited, and Medartis has insourced sterile packaging in Basel. The only supplier-related risks are the expiring NX nail distribution agreement (2027, being replaced by Titan Nail), single-site concentration of precision production in Basel (the May 2023 ransomware attack halted production for a week and cut H1 2023 sales by about 3%), and the CADskills supplier dispute (CHF 1.9m of legal and transaction costs in H1 2026).

1b

External tailwinds

7 answers
C-0172C-0354C-0460C-0868C-3268C-3758C-4376
C-01

To what extent is end-market demand changing, structurally or cyclically, and with how much visibility? (A cyclical swing counts when it has two-plus years of visibility.)

B726th of 36

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement. Demand is structurally strong and, for Medartis specifically, accelerating, with high visibility over two-plus years. The acceleration does not come from the end market speeding up; the market grows at a steady rate. It comes from Medartis creating a new market (thumb-base joint replacement with TOUCH in the US) and taking share in hand and wrist. The cyclical element is small and mostly negative at the margin (US elective softness, insurer pre-authorisation friction), while one cyclical positive in H1 2026 (winter trauma in Europe) is temporary.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • Structural rate. Extremities is the fastest-growing orthopaedic subsegment at about 9% a year, against 3% to 5% for general orthopaedics and trauma and about 3.5% for large joints (Morgan Stanley, via the Business Foundations digest). Drivers are ageing, active older adults, and procedure migration to ambulatory surgery centres. Trauma and hand surgery is largely non-discretionary: patients can defer a broken wrist for weeks, not quarters.
  • Medartis-specific new demand. TOUCH is a dual-mobility thumb carpometacarpal (CMC 1) prosthesis that replaces trapeziectomy (bone removal), the US standard of care. It received FDA premarket approval in July 2025, a new US procedure (CPT) code, Medicare transitional pass-through payment from 1 January 2026, and Australian reimbursement listing at about CHF 1,300 from 1 July 2026. Global TOUCH volume grew 40% to 43,000 units in 2025. US unit guidance for 2026 moved from 1,000 (CEO's first-year projection) to 1,200 to 1,800, and more than 2,000 US surgeries were performed by October 2026. This is demand created by an approval and payment decision; the sector digest's rule that revenue lags such decisions by about a year points to the bulk of the reimbursement benefit landing in 2027.
  • Cyclical negatives. US hip and knee growth slowed to 1.7% in Q3 2026 (Jefferies) on out-of-pocket cost sensitivity; insurer pre-authorisation denials rose in 2026; hospital administrators are tightening (the Bonesupport precedent of Q2 2026). Hand and trauma are far less exposed than elective joints, but a CMC 1 arthroplasty is an elective procedure, so TOUCH is more exposed to this than the plating franchise.
  • Cyclical positive that will reverse. Extreme winter weather in DACH and Poland lifted Q1 2026 trauma volumes; the Change Forces report itself warns that front-loaded H1 trauma could flatter the underlying rate. This creates a tougher H1 2027 comparison in EMEA (58% of sales).

What it means for the model. Model the market at about 9%, Medartis's core plating franchise at low-to-mid teens organic, and TOUCH/KeriMedical as a separate, faster line whose US element is reimbursement-gated. Do not extrapolate H1 2026 EMEA (17.7%) into H1 2027.

C-03

To what extent is a disruption force strengthening or being disproved, and in which direction does it act on the company?

D5420th of 36

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement. The disruption force most often cited against Medartis, robotics lock-out by Stryker and others, is being largely disproved in small-bone extremities; the forces strengthening are patient-specific 3D printing, digital planning and ambulatory surgery centre migration, and each cuts both ways. Net, disruption is neutral to slightly positive for Medartis over four years, because it has bought or built a position in each (CADskills, CMX, sterile and compact kits, Titan Nail).

Reasoning and evidence.

  • Robotics. The Risks report rated "robotics adoption by Stryker and DePuy" a high-plausibility failure (Mako shoulder). The later evidence (Arthrex sales manager expert call, July 2026; Morgan Stanley) says heavy robotic consoles give marginal benefit in small-joint trauma and penetration in extremities is minimal. Mako shoulder is a large-joint shoulder arthroplasty system, not a hand/wrist one. Treat this as a low-probability, long-dated threat.
  • Patient-specific 3D printing. Competitors price custom implants at parity with standard systems (Bernstein); hospitals are building point-of-care printing (Rambam/EOS/PTC, July 2026); Materialise and SINTX are expanding. This threatens the high-inventory consignment model in complex CMF and reconstruction. Medartis bought CADskills (Class III certified, May 2026) and runs CMX; this is a defensive and offensive position but immaterial to revenue for two years (US FDA clearance for CADskills products takes more than a year).
  • Ambulatory surgery centres. Migration of hand and foot procedures is a tailwind for compact, pre-sterilised kits (ASC sterilisation cost falls from about USD 4,500 to USD 1,000 per procedure with two-tray systems), and Medartis's Titan Nail procedure kit fits it; it is a threat where Acumed's fixed-price ASCent package undercuts per-implant pricing.
  • Bioresorbable magnesium screws (Bioretec RemeOs, FDA De Novo 2023) are a niche, paediatric and foot-and-ankle threat, years away from scale.
C-04

To what extent is political or regulatory change under way, and how does it bear on the company?

C+6011th of 36

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement. Political and regulatory change is large and its net sign is turning from negative (2025) to positive (2027). The negative was US tariffs; the positive is the reimbursement architecture now built around TOUCH in the US and Australia, the end of Australian price cuts, and a planned easing of the EU Medical Device Regulation. The residual negatives are slow-burning reimbursement pressure in Europe.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • US tariffs. Duties on Swiss devices moved from 10% (April 2025) to 39% (August to November 2025) to 15% (December 2025); management expects about 12.5% on US shipments. The cost was CHF 3.0m in FY2025 (0.9 points of gross margin) and is guided at about CHF 4.0m in FY2026 (about 1.2 points of sales), front-loaded into H1 2026 because tariffs sit in inventory for 6 to 10 months. Mitigation: Project Flash moves US-bound production to Warsaw, Indiana (70% of US volume local by end-2026, about 80% by mid-2027); selective US price increases of about 3% fund roughly a third of the burden; KeriMedical's French-made TOUCH carries the lower EU rate. After the 20 February 2026 IEEPA ruling, refunds are flowing across the sector; Medartis carries a CHF 4.4m tariff refund receivable at June 2026 that exceeded tariffs incurred in the period. Mechanically, 2027 should carry perhaps CHF 1m to 1.5m of tariff cost against CHF 4.0m in 2026, a gross margin tailwind of roughly 0.8 points, plus a one-off cash inflow when the refund is collected.
  • Reimbursement creating the TOUCH market. US CPT code; Medicare pass-through status from 1 January 2026 (the device is paid above the standard outpatient rate, which lowers the hospital administrator's objection); Australian listing from 1 July 2026. These are the single most important regulatory positives.
  • Australia. Government cuts of 12%, 5% and 5% (a cumulative 20% to 25% hit to Australian revenue over three years) ended in July 2025, so the 10% underlying volume growth now converts to sales.
  • EU MDR. Compliance cost remains high, but the Class III deadline is extended to 31 December 2027 and the Commission proposed a revision in late 2025 to improve predictability and remove the five-year certificate cap. It is also a barrier that hurts smaller challengers more than Medartis (CEO Daniellot of Straumann made the same point for dental).
  • Negatives. Germany's hybrid DRG system restrains lower-extremity hospital economics; Swiss hospital reimbursement is declining; Thailand imposed price cuts; Brazil's private insurers consolidated and tightened approvals in 2024 (Brazil sales fell 27% in H1 2024), and Medartis keeps a CHF 0.2m provision for historical Brazilian compliance investigations. A forced-labour tariff of 10% to 12.5% on medtech from 60 economies took effect on 24 July 2026 (Straumann Earnings Review); its relevance to Medartis's flows is unclear.
C-08

To what extent is a key driver at the industry or sector level accelerating the company's demand?

C+6811th of 36

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement. Yes, but the accelerating driver is narrow and sector-specific rather than broad: the payment and approval decision that has created a US market for thumb-base arthroplasty, together with the steady migration of hand and foot procedures to surgery centres. The broad industry driver (extremities growth of about 9%) is steady, not accelerating.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • The sector digest's investor rule applies precisely: "for a small maker with one consumable, a trial, approval or payment decision creates the market; revenue lags each approval by about a year". TOUCH has its approval (July 2025), code and pass-through (January 2026) and Australian listing (July 2026). The US adoption numbers (1,200 to 1,800 to more than 2,000 surgeries) show the driver working ahead of plan.
  • The 9 November 2026 sector insight adds the caveat: in US hospitals in 2026 the administrator, not the surgeon or payer, is the gate; value analysis committee approval takes up to four months per hospital.
  • Surgery-centre migration (US joint procedures in centres projected above 50% by 2030) favours nimble specialists with compact sets (sector insight of 10 October 2026, naming Medartis).
C-32

How is customer demand behaving: to what extent does it support the thesis, and how fragile is that support?

B687th of 36

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement. Surgeon demand strongly supports the thesis: TOUCH adoption is running well ahead of plan, US hand-surgeon training is on track, and exclusive distributors are growing fast. The fragility lies with the buyer, not the user: hospital administrators (value analysis committees taking up to four months per hospital), GPO contracts, insurers' pre-authorisation rules, and in Europe reimbursement tariffs. In the US the user (surgeon) is pulling while the payer and buyer are slowing.

Evidence. US TOUCH surgeries above 2,000 by October 2026 against an original 1,200 plan; more than 180 US surgeons trained in H1 2026 against a 330 target; Medartis contract coverage of about 62% of US high-margin volume through GPOs; Medicare pass-through status eases the administrator's objection; Bonesupport's Q2 2026 stall (sequential US growth 2% when large hospitals imposed cost containment, then +39% in Q3) is the sector's dated precedent for how administrator friction creates a temporary pause without loss of demand. European demand: EMEA +17.7% organic in H1 2026, helped by winter trauma; market share gains in the UK, Spain and Germany despite strikes.

C-37

What are regulators and political actors doing, does it help or hurt the company, and how fragile is that position?

C5813th of 36

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement. Net, regulators are now helping: the FDA's PMA, CMS's pass-through status and the new CPT code created the TOUCH market and raised the bar for copycats; Australia ended its price cuts and listed TOUCH; the EU is easing MDR. Political actors hurt through tariffs, which are being engineered away by local production. Hospital and payer regulators in Germany, Switzerland and Brazil exert slow pressure. The fragile parts: pass-through status is temporary by design (typically two to three years in US Medicare), so the TOUCH reimbursement uplift has a time limit; tariff policy can change suddenly (10% to 39% to 15% within nine months in 2025).

C-43

How large is the realistic total addressable market relative to the company, and what rate of growth does it allow for over the mid term?

A763rd of 36

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement. The realistic serviceable market is CHF 5bn to 6bn (CHF 4bn to 5bn in extremities and CMF, plus over CHF 800m added by KeriMedical's arthroplasty and NeoOrtho's LATAM value segment), growing at about 8% to 9%. Medartis's share is about 5% to 6%. That allows mid-term revenue growth of 15% to 20% a year for at least five years without heroic share assumptions, provided US share rises from about 2% towards the 5% management aims for.

Arithmetic. Market at CHF 5.5bn growing 9% reaches about CHF 7.8bn by 2030. Medartis at CHF 340m in 2026 growing 17% reaches about CHF 640m in 2030, about 8% share. Each additional point of US upper-extremities share is worth roughly CHF 30m to 40m of sales at current market size (US is close to 60% of the global extremities market). Sub-markets: global CMF CHF 1.5bn to 1.8bn; LATAM value CHF 450m; CMC 1 arthroplasty in the US is a new market whose size depends on conversion from trapeziectomy (large: thumb-base arthritis is common in older women), so TOUCH's ceiling is not the binding constraint in this horizon; surgeon training and hospital approval speed are.

1c

Overall external picture

2 answers
C-0672C-1060
C-06

Where is the company's own revenue and demand cycle, and to what extent is it turning?

B+725th of 36

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement. Medartis is in the early-to-middle phase of an up-cycle that has three overlapping legs: the US channel reset paying off, the TOUCH US launch, and a full set of new product launches. The cycle is not turning down; organic growth will very probably peak in H2 2026 or H1 2027 as several mechanical tailwinds coincide, then moderate in 2027 as comparisons harden. The question for the model is not "is it turning" but "how far does it fall back from the peak".

Reasoning and evidence.

  • Organic growth path: FY2024 11.7%, FY2025 15.7% (18% excluding the Florida disruption, per management), H1 2026 17.0%, FY2026 guided 17% to 19% and, per the 2 November statement, likely to be exceeded.
  • Why H2 2026 is mechanically strong. (a) KeriMedical was consolidated in July 2025, so from July 2026 its sales, including the TOUCH US ramp, move from "acquired" into the organic base; (b) H2 2025 US growth was depressed by the termination of the largest Florida distributor (nearly 10% of US sales) in mid-2025, so H2 2026 US faces an easy comparison; (c) Australian TOUCH reimbursement started 1 July 2026; (d) US surgeon training accelerates (180 trained in H1 2026 against a 330 target).
  • Why 2027 comparisons harden. TOUCH US annualises from H2 2026; EMEA laps winter-trauma-boosted H1 2026; Florida is fully rebuilt (management expected full integration within 12 months of mid-2025).
  • Sub-cycles turning down. APAC slowed to 5.5% organic in H1 2026 (Japan wrist loss to the former distributor, delayed elbow and clavicle approvals, Thai price cuts); LATAM organic 9.7%; lower extremities 10.4% (from 15.1% in FY2025). Upper extremities (21.1%) and the US (27.4%) now carry the group.

What it means for the model. A reasonable shape is organic growth near 19% to 20% in H2 2026, mid-to-high teens in 2027 if TOUCH scales, and low-to-mid teens by 2028, with the mix ever more dependent on the US and on TOUCH.

C-10

Taking each sector and industry thematic driver in turn, and then all of the above together: how strong are the external headwinds and how strong are the tailwinds at value-chain, industry and sector level, and what is the overall external change picture?

C+6011th of 36

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement. The external picture is moderately positive and improving into 2027, with the tailwinds concentrated in demand and regulation and the headwinds concentrated in currency, competition and the market regime. At value-chain level the picture is favourable (demand, reimbursement, suppliers); at industry level mixed (rational but intensifying competition, administrator gating); at sector and market level negative (Swiss medtech de-rating, franc, discount rate, small-cap fragility). The external forces are strong enough to carry high-teens organic growth into 2027; they are not strong enough on their own to deliver the margin expansion the sell-side models, which depends on internal execution and on the franc.

Thematic drivers, one by one.

  • Worries clearing at reimbursed franchises (sector theme 1): applies partly; Medartis's worries (US channel, tariffs) are clearing operationally, but its multiple is already near the top of its sector, so the "multiple rises at a low base" mechanism does not apply. Neutral.
  • Premium products the patient pays for (theme 2): does not apply; Medartis is hospital- and insurer-paid. Neutral.
  • Consumables becoming standard of care through trials and payment decisions (theme 3): applies strongly to TOUCH. Tailwind.
  • Devices for weight-loss injections (theme 4): not relevant (GLP-1 drugs may enlarge the joint-replacement pool, irrelevant to hand trauma). Neutral.
  • Industry: tariffs proving manageable where production can move (1 October 2026 insight): applies, with the explicit warning that Medartis is the type of small company where CHF 4m of tariff cost breaks a margin promise. Headwind turning tailwind.
  • Geography: unhedged Swiss exporters report the currency first: applies fully. Headwind.

Overall balance. Tailwinds: about 9% structural market growth, a reimbursement-created TOUCH market, the end of Australian cuts, tariff relief from 2027, MDR easing, peer disruption. Headwinds: franc strength with no hedge, rising product-level competition in wrist, foot and CMF, GPO and administrator pricing gates (about 1% to 2% annual price-downs), and a risk-off regime that punishes small, highly rated growth stocks.

Internal change forces/risks

Memo topic 2, : the Rater's score

Behind the rating as a whole

Group judgement

Internal change forces

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group. The rating above stands for it, so it carries no number of its own.

This is a genuine "massive corporate change" case in the Wisdom Library's sense: a new animal-type operator, a new strategy, three acquisitions and a rebuilt channel, all arriving within 24 months. The evidence of action is unusually concrete (distributor review to replacement in three months, machines shipped to Indiana within weeks of the 39% tariff, NeoOrtho integrated in ten months, two raise-and-beat years). The weaknesses are the ones typical of a company changing this much at once: the US leadership seat is empty again, the balance sheet has been spent, and the earnings base is cluttered with non-core items. For the model, internal change forces support sustained high-teens organic growth and a gross margin back towards 82% to 83% as tariffs fade and TOUCH mix rises; operating margin expansion and cash conversion should be modelled as conditional on a visible decision by management (most likely signalled in the FY2027 guidance on 2 March 2027) rather than as automatic.

2a

Internal risks

6 answers
Judgement44C-1462C-8638C-5960C-8144D-0340
Group judgement

Risks

C+4411th of 36

Layer 1 · Risks

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

The risk profile has improved since the Risks report's June 2026 "false friend" verdict: its two load-bearing failure modes (US channel attrition and gross-margin erosion) were disconfirmed by H1 2026 (US +27.4%, core gross margin +0.8 points), and receivable days held flat. What remains is the cash-conversion leg of the false-friend hypothesis (FCF negative, NWC 47% of sales) and a set of concentrated risks: one product (TOUCH) carrying the US and 2027 growth, one high-cost Swiss site, one unhedged currency, and a vacant US leadership seat. The largest risk to an investor is not operational; it is that a highly rated, thinly traded stock meets its first deceleration in a risk-off market. For Set 2: keep the false-friend test alive on cash, not on margins; build a downside case with growth at low teens in 2027 and flat margins; and remember that the share-price downside in that case is 25% to 40%.

C-14

To what extent are standards improving in execution, operational measures, governance or cost discipline? (choices and actions)

D6217th of 36

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement. Standards are improving clearly in commercial execution, integration and cost control, and less clearly in cash discipline and accounting conservatism. The improvements are visible in outcomes, not just in language.

Reasoning and evidence (improving).

  • Integration on schedule: Cold Fusion merged the Medartis Brazil and NeoOrtho back offices in Curitiba within ten months, completed in early 2026, with synergies from 2027.
  • Cost discipline: core opex fell to 73.1% of sales in FY2025 (73.8% in FY2024) and to 72.4% in H1 2026 (73.0%); headquarters costs were held during the CEO transition.
  • Channel: exclusive US distributors grew sales 46% year to date against 16% for multiline partners; the network was rebuilt to 65 partners; US organic growth reached 27.4% in H1 2026.
  • Collections: H2 2025 cash inflow came mainly from reducing receivable days (Hackel); receivable days held flat at 59 in H1 2026, which argues against the channel-stuffing hypothesis in the Risks report (Failure #11).
  • Governance: compliance training 92% completion, no fines in 2025; long-term pay moved to performance shares with a cash-flow test; board minimum shareholding introduced; on-time-in-full delivery 93.15%; engagement (Glint) 79 in April 2026 against 71 in 2023/24.

Reasoning and evidence (not improving or mixed).

  • Cash: free cash flow fell from CHF 21.0m (FY2024) to CHF 9.3m (FY2025) to CHF -10.1m (H1 2026); working capital absorbed CHF 25.0m in H1 2026.
  • Accounting conservatism: capitalised development rose to CHF 12.5m (2025) from CHF 9.0m; the core-adjustment list is long (see C-54); no dedicated internal audit function; the same auditor since 2004.
  • Quality: products recalled in December 2025 after a sterilizer exceeded water bioburden limits (minor field action, no authority-mandated recall).
C-86

What negative corporate change developments are present/plausible?

C-3811th of 36

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement. The negatives are real and clustered around three themes: leadership gaps in the two growth regions, a cash squeeze created by the investment programme, and execution load. None is thesis-breaking on its own; together they make the 18-month path more fragile than the growth headline suggests.

Present.

  • US head ad interim (the finance director) and no permanent LATAM head, as of the H1 2026 presentation.
  • Cash CHF 25.8m against H1 2026 free cash flow of CHF -10.1m; capex tracking above the CHF 25m guide; working capital at about 47% of trailing sales (FactSet) and inventory at about 36% of sales.
  • At least six concurrent change programmes: leadership rebuild, US channel, Project Flash, KeriMedical, NeoOrtho, CADskills, plus Japan direct and LATAM country entries (the CEO: "we have some balls in the air").
  • Japan wrist share lost to the former distributor; elbow and clavicle approvals delayed.
  • Capitalised development rising faster than sales (CHF 9.0m to CHF 12.5m, +39%).
  • Founder and former CEO selling.

Plausible.

  • An equity raise or more debt if a further acquisition arises or if the NWC build continues.
  • The NX-to-Titan Nail transition slipping in 2027.
  • Overcapacity at Warsaw and the French plants weighing on gross margin if growth slows (the Leadership digest's expert calls already cite Warsaw overcapacity).
  • Convertible conversion diluting EPS by roughly 7% to 8% (see C-19).
C-59

How holistic and congruent is the culture, with alignment across the total system (as at Lifco, Avanza or Ryanair)? Such that the goals, strategy, culture, execution, decisions are congruent, holistic and symbiotic?

C+6010th of 36

The Investment Analyst's heading How holistic and congruent is the culture, with alignment across the total system?

Judgement. Congruence is improving but not yet holistic. Goals (growth with profitability), strategy (multi-brand, training-led), pay (sales growth, opex ratio, EBITDA margin, relative TSR and cash flow), and culture work (WE-IT-I values, high-performance team workshops, engagement up from 71 to 79) point the same way. The gaps: the guidance habit of holding margins at "high teens" is not fully congruent with a pay scheme now rewarding cash and opex ratio; three brands are deliberately run separately (KeriMedical stand-alone, NeoOrtho separate sales forces), which is sensible commercially but limits one culture; integration-related turnover rose in 2025.

D-03

In relation to the differences between the forecasts over the next 18 months to four years, vs. the last four to six years' financials, how diffuse or concentrated are the key drivers that generate the differences?

F408th of 9

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement. The level of revenue is diffuse; everything that makes the forecast different from the past is concentrated. Revenue grows at the same rate as before (about 16% a year in both periods), on a broad base. The differences from the past (an EBITDA margin above 19% for the first time, EBIT margin rising from 9% to 14%, EPS multiplying, cash turning positive) come from four drivers, and behind them from one product in one country. In descending order of weight: (1) US TOUCH volume and price; (2) management's decision on the selling-cost ratio; (3) the tax rate; (4) the depreciation ratio after the investment peak. Two are operating and visible every half-year; two are accounting outcomes visible once a year. A fifth, the franc on unhedged intercompany loans, generates no difference on average and a large share of the variance.

How concentrated, in numbers.

Measure US TOUCH's share Basis
Group revenue, FY2027 about 9% (CHF 37m of 394m) Set 2's 7,000 procedures at USD 6,500
Increase in group revenue, FY2027 about 38% (CHF 21m of 56m; six of the 16.5 points of growth) Same
Increase in group revenue, FY2028 about 27% (CHF 16m of 60m) 10,000 procedures, price held
Increase in group EBITDA, FY2027 about 60% (CHF 8m of 14m) My assumption of a 40% contribution after agent commission and field support; an estimate, not a disclosure
Group revenue growth in FY2027 if US TOUCH only holds its late-2026 monthly rate about 12% to 13% instead of 16.5% Exit rate of about 400 a month

So a product that is 9% of sales carries about 40% of next year's growth and, on my estimate, more than half of next year's profit growth. And it is concentrated further along four dimensions at once: one country, one payer decision (pass-through to end-2028), one price point that we cannot verify, and one channel whose leadership is unclear.

Where the revenue is diffuse, and why that still matters. EMEA is about 56% of sales, grows in the low teens on an installed base of trained surgeons, and would continue under average management. APAC and LATAM add a further 20%. This is what makes revenue robust: if US TOUCH stalled completely at today's run-rate, group growth would still be about 12% to 13% in FY2027. But as D-02 showed, growth of that kind does not move EPS; the diffuse part of the business protects the top line, not the earnings difference.

The non-operating drivers are concentrated too. Tax contributes about one-third of forecast EPS growth to FY2029 and is a single number set by where inventory sits and which subsidiaries make profit. The margin contribution depends on one ratio, selling and distribution cost to sales, falling from about 37% to about 34%, when its last reading was a rise.

Against peers. Bonesupport is more concentrated (one product) and is valued as such; Medacta and Straumann are far more diffuse by product. Medartis has moved, in eighteen months, from a diffuse plating company to one whose incremental economics resemble a single-product launch, without the single-product company's clean cost base. The Set 1 answer on perimeter (C-79) called the group "moderately broad and widening"; that is true of its complexity and false of its drivers. It has the forecasting disadvantages of both: many moving parts for costs, one moving part for growth.

What concentration does to predictability. Concentration is not only a risk. A concentrated driver that is disclosed and counted (US TOUCH procedures) is easier to track than a diffuse one, and this one is running ahead of plan. The problem is that the concentrated drivers below EBITDA are not disclosed in advance, and the price of the concentrated operating driver is not disclosed at all.

Internal corporate change forces

C-11

To what extent have the chief executive, or other key leaders (e.g. finance director, operations director, board, division heads, etc.) or other key actors (e.g. activists, major shareholders) changed, and what does the change signal?

D5623rd of 36

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement. Change has been near-total and, taken as a whole, signals professionalisation along the Straumann template: a new chief executive, finance director, R&D head, HR head and board finance expert, most drawn from or tied to Straumann Group. The signal is positive on intent and capability. It is offset by one new and important negative: the US President hired in January 2025 to lead the most important growth region appears to have left, with the finance director covering the role ad interim during the TOUCH launch, and there is no permanent Latin America head. Leadership change is therefore still in progress, not complete as the FY2025 Earnings Review suggested.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • Chief executive Matthias Schupp from November 2024 (17 years at Straumann; built Neodent into the leading value dental implant brand; ran Straumann LATAM for ten years). He was hired under chairman Marco Gadola, his former Straumann chief executive.
  • Finance director Dr Peter Hackel from 1 January 2026 (former Straumann Group finance director, McKinsey; six-month overlap with Dirk Kirsten). Hackel's remit is explicitly cash conversion.
  • EVP R&D Marc Ammann (May/June 2025, internal, 17 years); CHRO Caroline Knoeri (August 2025; the post changed twice in 18 months); US President David Thoni (January 2025).
  • Board: Yang Xu (former Straumann finance director, now CFO of JDE Peet's) elected April 2026 to the audit committee, replacing Jennifer Dean after two years; Martha Shadan added 2024 for US expertise.
  • The negative. The H1 2026 presentation lists Hackel as US head ad interim and the Latin America leader as "to be announced". A finance director running the US commercial organisation while also driving a cash-conversion programme, during the region's most important product launch, is a stretch. The Risks report named "sudden leadership changes at the US Presidency level" as Failure #8.
  • Key actors. Founder Thomas Straumann (39.9% of votes, vice-chairman since 1998) sold 188,994 shares in 2025 (-3.4% of his holding); former chief executive Willi Miesch sold 53,577 (-8.7%). Schupp raised his holding from 1,800 to 4,280 shares. The founder's trimming is small but is the wrong direction for the anchor holder of a stock that re-rated 56% in 2025.

What it signals for the model. Expect execution discipline in cost and cash (Hackel's KPIs, cash-flow metrics in pay), a Straumann-style multi-brand playbook, and continued US momentum carried by the distributor network and TOUCH specialists rather than by a US chief. A permanent US appointment is a dated near-term catalyst; its absence beyond H1 2027 would be a warning.

C-12

To what extent have the strategy or the strategic priorities changed, and how well is the change articulated and resourced? (choices)

C+667th of 36

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement. Strategy has changed materially and is well articulated: from a single-brand Swiss premium fixation company to "Three Brands, One Group" (Medartis premium fixation, KeriMedical small-joint arthroplasty, NeoOrtho value implants, plus CADskills patient-specific implants), run through six must-win priority clusters each sponsored by an executive. The most important recent shift is in emphasis: the chief executive says the group is "no longer solely focusing on growth", and cash flow and operating leverage now feed variable pay. Resourcing is strong in people and capacity and weak in balance-sheet headroom.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • Six clusters: Accelerating US; KeriMedical sales; Value strategy; Regional share gains; Innovation and digitalisation; Improving cash flow. "From Head to Toe" remains the scope; the CEO says Medartis will selectively lead in upper extremities and CMF rather than compete in commoditised lower-extremity segments.
  • Pay alignment: short-term incentive weighted 50% net sales growth, 30% opex ratio, 20% EBITDA margin (2025 payout factor 163.8%); long-term plan moved to 100% performance share units split between relative TSR (against SPI Extra) and a cash flow metric; minimum shareholding introduced 2026.
  • Resourcing: capex of CHF 25.5m in 2025 and CHF 17.9m in H1 2026 (guided about CHF 25m for 2026, now tracking above); five plants; Orlando training office and the M.A.R.C. Institute in Miami (acquired September 2026) for US surgeon education; ten dedicated TOUCH field experts and 45 key-opinion-leader trainers.
  • Constraint: cash fell from CHF 138.7m (end-2024) to CHF 25.8m (June 2026) after CHF 120.8m of net M&A and the capex programme; H1 2026 free cash flow was CHF -10.1m. The strategy is resourced to the edge of the balance sheet.

Tension to carry into the model. The strategy asks for growth everywhere at once (US, TOUCH, LATAM value, CADskills, Japan, Mexico, Colombia, Argentina, Chile) while the pay scheme now also rewards cash and opex ratio. The likely resolution is that management slows discretionary spend (the UK subsidiary already delays non-essential marketing and education events) rather than slowing growth investments in the US.

C-13

To what extent has financial guidance been raised, or have mid-term targets or capital-markets-day ambitions been set or raised? (choices)

A744th of 36

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement. Guidance has been raised twice in a row at the half-year, and the year-end signal for 2026 points to a third beat. The pattern is a top-line one: organic growth ranges are raised, the margin guide is held at "high teens at constant currency" every time. No formal group mid-term financial targets exist; the stated ambition is US organic growth above 20% a year.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • FY2025: initial 13% to 15%, raised to 14% to 16% at H1 2025, delivered 15.7%.
  • FY2026: initial 16% to 18% (March 2026), raised to 17% to 19% at H1 2026 (18 August 2026); on 2 November 2026 management said the group is well positioned to exceed its 2026 financial forecast.
  • Operating guidance raised in parallel: US TOUCH units 1,200 to 1,800 in 2026; more than 2,000 surgeries by October.
  • Margin guidance: "high teens" core EBITDA margin at constant currency, unchanged across FY2025 and FY2026. Management's own words frame it as protecting reinvestment ("reflecting planned growth investments").

What it means for the model. Assume the top-line guide is a floor, not a midpoint. Do not assume a margin beat; the guidance architecture is designed to convert excess growth into reinvestment and keep reported margin in the 18% to 19% range until management chooses to let it rise. The first FY2027 guidance (2 March 2027) is the key test of whether management lets margin through.

C-15

To what extent is the company reducing its perimeter or composition, through divestments, simplification or exits from low-return units? (actions)

D5221st of 36

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement. There is real but small-scale pruning, sensible in every case, but the net direction of the perimeter is expansion, not reduction. Simplification is a secondary theme, not a driver.

Evidence.

  • Exited third-party contract manufacturing inherited with Nextremity Solutions (NSI) in Warsaw: revenue fell from CHF 5.2m (FY2024) to CHF 1.8m (FY2025), freeing capacity for Project Flash. The acquired NSI technologies were fully impaired (CHF 17.9m) in 2024.
  • Divested NeoOrtho's legacy hip business (about CHF 1.4m of sales) in H2 2025.
  • Replacing the third-party NX nail with the proprietary Titan Nail ahead of the 2027 contract expiry.
  • Terminated the largest Florida distributor (nearly 10% of US sales) and about 20 of 57 US partners.
  • Exited China in Q3 2022 (pre-Schupp).
  • Against this: three acquisitions since 2025 (KeriMedical 100%, NeoOrtho 51%, CADskills 100%) plus the M.A.R.C. Institute, new country entries in LATAM, and production sites expanding from one to five or six.

For the model. The pruning removes about CHF 3m to 5m of low-margin revenue from the FY2024 and FY2025 base (a small drag on reported growth, a small lift to gross margin) and nothing further of size is expected.

C-16

To what extent is the company entering new geographic or product markets, and how sensibly? (actions)

C-6015th of 36

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement. Entry into new markets is extensive and, individually, sensible, because almost every move draws on an existing customer, channel or capability (the "core versus adjacency" test). Collectively it is a lot to do at once for a company of CHF 340m of sales, which is the main risk.

Assessment by move, nearest to farthest from the core.

  • US hand and wrist share gain (core): Medartis has about 2% of US upper extremities against a market that is nearly 60% of the global extremities opportunity. Most sensible move, highest return.
  • TOUCH in the US (close adjacency): same surgeon (hand surgeon), same channel, new product; cross-sell of the APTUS plating range to TOUCH accounts is the stated gateway. Very sensible.
  • Japan direct (core product, new channel): CMF up more than 50% after going direct, but upper extremities lost share to the former distributor; elbow and clavicle approvals delayed. Sensible, poorly timed.
  • LATAM value (NeoOrtho; midrange adjacency): a dual-brand strategy for public tenders (75% of the regional market; CHF 450m value segment) that the CEO has run before at Neodent. Plausible, dilutive to gross margin (NeoOrtho at about 65%), expanding to Colombia, Mexico, Argentina and Chile in 2026/27. The CEO says NeoOrtho is not for the US.
  • CADskills patient-specific 3D printing (midrange): complements CMX and CMF; immaterial revenue; US clearance takes over a year.
  • Shoulder and ankle indications: broaden the portfolio against stronger incumbents (Stryker in shoulder, Zimmer/Paragon 28 in foot and ankle); the least differentiated push.
C-17

To what extent is the company raising prices in real terms (above cost inflation), and are the rises real, merely nominal, or so far only latent or plausible and increasingly noted by management? (actions)

D4820th of 36

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

The Investment Analyst's heading To what extent is the company raising prices in real terms, and are the rises real, merely nominal, or so far only latent?

Judgement. In the core plating business price rises are nominal at best and mostly defensive; the real pricing gain is coming through mix, from TOUCH, which carries a premium price backed by reimbursement codes. So pricing power is real in one product, latent in the brand, and absent in the trauma plate tender market.

Evidence.

  • US: selective price increases of about 3% in early 2026 to offset tariffs, but about 60% of US sales run on multi-year GPO contracts and the increases fund only about a third of the tariff burden. This is a nominal pass-through of a cost, not a real rise.
  • GPOs and hospital consortia impose roughly 2% annual price pressure and automatic price-downs of 1% to 2% (Morgan Stanley; 10-K sources); public tenders in Europe and Latin America push prices down.
  • Australia: cumulative cuts of 20% to 25% were absorbed via 10% volume growth; Thailand mandated cuts.
  • Premium is held: Medartis claims a 10% to 15% price premium over generalist trauma suppliers, and expert commentary says implant average selling prices have been stable.
  • TOUCH: core gross margin rose 0.8 points to 81.5% in H1 2026 despite 1.2 points of currency and tariff drag; the company attributes this to TOUCH's premium pricing and to internalising the KeriMedical margin. Australian private-list reimbursement at about CHF 1,300 per unit and US pass-through status anchor that price.

For the model. Model like-for-like price at roughly -1% to +1% a year in plating and positive mix from TOUCH and from proprietary products (Titan Nail), offset by NeoOrtho dilution in LATAM.

C-18

To what extent is the company stepping up mergers and acquisitions, and how strategically sensible, disciplined and well integrated are they? (actions)

B628th of 36

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement. M&A has stepped up sharply and is strategically sensible; integration so far is on schedule; discipline on price is hard to judge because valuations are not disclosed, and the group's earlier record (NSI) was poor. The KeriMedical deal is the best of the set because it secures ownership of the product driving the growth story.

Evidence.

  • KeriMedical: remaining 53.03% bought for CHF 99m in cash in July 2025, triggered by FDA approval; previously an associate. Consolidated KeriMedical grew 30% and contributed CHF 14.1m of sales in H2 2025; the remeasurement of the prior stake produced a CHF 14.5m non-core gain; inventory step-up amortisation was a CHF 8.8m non-core charge. Kept as a stand-alone brand with founders on an advisory board; capacity doubling at Besançon from Q4 2026.
  • NeoOrtho: 51% in May 2025, with a CHF 15.4m liability for the remaining 49% in 2028/29; contributed CHF 8.3m of FY2025 sales and CHF 7.2m in H1 2026; EBITDA-accretive but gross-margin dilutive; integration (Cold Fusion) completed on schedule.
  • CADskills: 100% (signed March 2026, closed May 2026), founder retained with sales-based earn-out; CHF 1.9m of transaction and supplier-dispute legal costs in H1 2026 (non-core).
  • M.A.R.C. Institute, Miami (September 2026): a surgical education centre, not a revenue business.
  • History: Nextremity Solutions (2022, CHF 36.1m) ended in a CHF 17.9m impairment in 2024 and the release of CHF 8.6m of contingent consideration; that deal pre-dates the present management.

For the model. No large deal is likely in the next 18 months given cash of CHF 25.8m, unless funded with equity or more debt; small tuck-ins and the 2028/29 NeoOrtho buy-out are the known calls on cash.

C-19

To what extent is the company making more use of its balance sheet (for investment, acquisitions or returns), or stepping up shareholder returns (a buyback starting or increasing, a special or progressive dividend), or is management increasingly mooting either? (actions)

F4228th of 36

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement. The balance sheet has been used fully, for acquisitions and capacity, and there is no room or intent to return capital. No dividend is paid, no buyback is planned, and employee plans are served from conditional capital. This is appropriate for the reinvestment opportunity, but it leaves the equity with no cash-return support and limited protection.

Evidence. Cash fell from CHF 138.7m to CHF 25.8m in 18 months; the CHF 115.8m convertible (April 2024, 3.0%, due 2031) funded the deals; CHF 12m of bank lines drawn, with more than CHF 50m undrawn at end-2025; net debt about CHF 84m excluding leases and the NeoOrtho put (about 1.5x trailing core EBITDA of about CHF 56m) or CHF 181m on FactSet's all-in definition. Two brokers see FCF of CHF 3m (FY2026) and CHF 10m (FY2027) before rising to CHF 30m (FY2028). Management mentions no buyback or dividend; the Business Foundations digest records free cash flow "fully reinvested in organic expansion and international M&A".

For the model. Share count can only rise: employee plans, and potential conversion of the convertible (conversion price not in the files; FactSet's 14.7m diluted shares against roughly 13.6m registered suggests about one million shares of potential dilution). Model both.

C-20

What other material positive corporate change forces are present/plausible?

–62

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement. Four further positives matter: the US surgeon-education infrastructure now being built, the shift to US-made product, the proprietary-product replacement programme, and the change in pay that ties management to cash and to relative shareholder return.

Evidence.

  • Education as a growth engine. IBRA trained 1,450 surgical professionals in 2025; the US programme targets 330 hand surgeons in 2026 (roughly 10% of the US hand-surgeon population); the Orlando training office and the M.A.R.C. Institute add the first US wet-lab centre. Management's thesis, backed by Straumann's ITI experience, is that surgeons adopt the system they train on.
  • Local manufacturing. Warsaw production gives a dollar cost base for US sales (a partial natural currency hedge) and removes tariff exposure.
  • Own IP replacing distributed IP. Titan Nail for NX; KeriMedical manufacturing margins now inside the group.
  • Pay and culture. Cash-flow and relative-TSR performance shares; "WE-IT-I" values and engagement up from 71 to 79; the Swiss Medtech Award 2026.
  • Plausible: a capital markets day setting mid-term targets (none announced); first-time sell-side coverage expansion (five named covering houses, only two visible in FactSet); a permanent US President with large-company experience.
C-87

To what extent have key controlling actors (e.g. activists, major shareholders) indicated scope for change in strategy/approach?

F4827th of 36

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement. They have not. The controlling shareholder, Thomas Straumann (39.9% of votes), co-founded the company, has sat on the board since 1998 and backed the hiring of a Straumann-trained team; there is no activist; the other blockholders (Nordflint Capital 8.7%, Endeavour Medtech Growth 5.7% to 6.4%, represented on the board by Damien Tappy) show no public agitation. The only signals are passive: small founder share sales and the defensive opting-out clause that removes the mandatory bid protection for minorities.

Implication. Strategy is stable and unlikely to be forced to change; a takeover is possible only with the founder's consent, and if it came, the opting-out clause allows a buyer to acquire control without bidding for the minority. This is neutral for the operating model and negative for minority-shareholder optionality.

C-21

Taken together, how strong, broad and well-resourced are the positive corporate changes under way?

B667th of 36

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement. The positive corporate change is strong and broad: a new, capable, Straumann-trained leadership team; a clear multi-brand strategy; a product (TOUCH) that the group now owns outright and that is beating its plan; a rebuilt US channel delivering 27% organic growth; local US production; and two consecutive raise-and-beat years. It is well resourced in people, products and capacity and under-resourced in cash and in senior US leadership. The change has already shown up in revenue (organic growth from 11.7% to 17%+) and gross margin (81.5%) but not yet in operating margin, cash flow or returns on capital, which is where the remaining proof lies.

Why this matters for Set 2. The change forces justify a model in which revenue growth stays well above the market for at least two years. They do not by themselves justify the margin and cash inflection the brokers model; that depends on management choosing to let operating leverage through, which the pay scheme now encourages but the guidance architecture ("high teens") has so far withheld.

C-22

What staircase of novel positive developments lies ahead: which new, dated things can the market look forward to within the next 6 months, in months 6 to 18, and in months 18 to 36; how novel is each; and how well-founded?

A662nd of 36

Layer 1 · Stock market narrative

Judgement. The staircase is well stocked for the next 18 months, with a mix of highly novel product and reimbursement milestones and mechanical margin steps, and it thins after that. The steps most likely to move the narrative are the FY2026 results with the first FY2027 guidance (March 2027), evidence that TOUCH volumes compound in the US in 2027, and visible gross-margin recovery as tariffs fall away. The biggest gap in the staircase is the US leadership appointment, which is needed but not yet dated.

Next 6 months (to May 2027).

  • FY2026 results, 2 March 2027: organic growth above 17% to 19% (well-founded, signalled on 2 November); first FY2027 guidance (novelty high: the first chance for management to signal margin release); full-year TOUCH US units, likely well above 2,000 (well-founded).
  • Project Flash at 70% of US volume made in Warsaw by end-2026 (well-founded, "on track"); collection of the CHF 4.4m tariff refund (moderately founded).
  • A permanent US President (novelty medium; foundation unknown; not dated).
  • US surgical education: Orlando training office and the M.A.R.C. Institute in Miami running (well-founded); 330 US hand surgeons trained in 2026 (on track at 180 in H1).
  • KeriMedical's new Besançon plant starting in Q4 2026 (capacity doubling; well-founded).
  • Australian TOUCH reimbursement annualising from July 2026 (well-founded).
  • AGM April 2027.

Months 6 to 18 (to May 2028).

  • H1 2027 results (August 2027): the first half in which TOUCH is wholly organic and tariffs largely gone; gross margin back towards 82% to 83% (moderately well-founded).
  • About 80% of US volume made locally by mid-2027 (well-founded).
  • Cold Fusion synergies in LATAM from 2027 (well-founded, size undisclosed).
  • Titan Nail replacing the NX nail before the 2027 contract expiry (moderately founded; transition risk).
  • Capex and set investment falling after the 2026 peak; FCF turning positive (moderately founded).
  • Japan elbow and clavicle approvals; LATAM entries in Colombia, Mexico, Argentina and Chile (low-to-moderate novelty, moderate foundation).
  • CADskills US FDA clearance (more than a year away; low-to-moderate foundation).
  • DePuy Synthes separation by mid-2027 (an external event that may free surgeon accounts).
  • A capital markets day or mid-term targets (not announced; would be highly novel).

Months 18 to 36 (to late 2029).

  • TOUCH US scaling to several thousand units a year, with APTUS plating cross-sold to TOUCH accounts (novel, moderately founded); a second US reimbursement milestone if pass-through status ends and a permanent payment rate is set (uncertain).
  • CMX digital navigation tools (2027 to 2028 roadmap; low foundation).
  • NeoOrtho remaining 49% bought in 2028/29 (CHF 15.4m; well-founded, low novelty).
  • Operating margin into the 20s (EBITDA) and positive FCF funding growth (founded only if the leverage appears).
  • Convertible maturity or conversion by 2031 (balance-sheet event).

Foundations

3

Value chain dynamics and industry structure

C6015th of 36

The Investment Analyst's group judgement: Industry and Value Chain

Behind the rating as a whole

Group judgement

Industry and Value Chain

C6015th of 36

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

The value chain is favourable around Medartis: a growing niche, a weak supplier side, rational large competitors, and regulators who have just created a protected market for its most important new product. The pressure point is the buyer: GPOs, hospital administrators and public tenders, which cap price, gate new products and favour the largest suppliers through bundled contracts. Medartis is advantaged with the surgeon and disadvantaged with the administrator; its growth model (training, exclusive distributors, a reimbursed differentiated product) is the right one for that structure. The empirical precedents say this is the phase in which specialist orthopaedic stocks outperform, and that the reversal comes with the first deceleration. For the model: price flat, share gains continuing, competitive cost rising, and a TOUCH reimbursement benefit that is real but time-limited.

3a

Value chain dynamics

6 answers
C-3268C-3362C-3448C-3758C-3855C-3966
C-32How is customer demand behaving: to what extent does it support the thesis, and how fragile is that support?B687th of 36full answer under 1b External tailwinds
C-33

To what extent are the route to market and the intermediaries helping or hindering the company, and how fragile is that?

C+6210th of 36

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement. The route to market has moved from a hindrance (2024/25) to a help (2026). The US exclusive-distributor model is working, direct markets in Europe are the most profitable channel, and Japan and Mexico are being converted to direct. Fragility is moderate: the US channel is a network of independent exclusive agencies whose loyalty depends on commissions and on a US leadership that is currently ad interim; the Japanese transition shows that a former partner can become a competitor.

Evidence. About 20 of 57 US partners replaced in 2025, the largest Florida distributor (nearly 10% of US sales) terminated, five new Florida distributors appointed with a sixth region and Orlando office, network now 65 partners; exclusive partners grew 46% against 16% for multiline. Excluding the Florida transition, FY2025 group growth would have been about 18% rather than 15.7%. Spain has grown for five years since going direct in 2021; Japan direct doubled CMF but lost wrist share. Distributor sales outside direct markets are lumpy (sets bought upfront, 60-day terms). Distribution and selling costs are large (37.7% of sales, see the Preface caution), and the Risks report treats a rise in that ratio as the first sign of channel decay; it should be tracked.

C-34

How are existing competitors behaving, does that help or hurt the company, and how fragile is the company's position against them?

D4827th of 36

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement. Competitor behaviour hurts at the margin: product parity is narrowing in wrist and elbow plating, the majors are entering Medartis's home market, and Acumed is attacking from the surgery-centre angle. It helps where peers are disrupted by their own integrations and reorganisations. Medartis's position is robust in Europe and in thumb-base arthroplasty and fragile in the US plating business, where it has neither the scale nor the contracts of the incumbents.

Evidence. Stryker Pangea (33 plates, first European cases mid-2026); Acumed Acu-Loc NEXT (first case at Duke, December 2025), ExsoMed, TECHFIT, ASCent; Globus elbow and 30% trauma growth; Zimmer Biomet integrating Paragon 28; Japan's former distributor. Helping: Zimmer's sales force reorganisation (it blamed knee underperformance on it), DePuy separation, Smith & Nephew restructuring; management says peer disruption creates account-conversion openings. TOUCH has a ten-year European clinical record and a five-year ODEP rating, a lead competitors would need years and a PMA to replicate.

C-37What are regulators and political actors doing, does it help or hurt the company, and how fragile is that position?C5813th of 36full answer under 1b External tailwinds
C-38

On whom in the value chain are disruption forces acting (demand, competition, regulators and political actors, suppliers, any other actor), how is each acting, and how fragile do they leave the company's position?

C5512th of 36

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement. The forces act mostly on buyers and on competitors' business models rather than on Medartis's core, leaving its position moderately robust.

  • Demand (hospitals, surgery centres): migration to ambulatory centres and administrator cost control push for compact, sterile, fixed-price solutions; Medartis is adapting (sterile kits, Titan Nail), Acumed is attacking with ASCent.
  • Competitors: robotics consolidates large-joint share (Stryker does two-thirds of its US knees robotically) but has little relevance in hand trauma; patient-specific printing lowers the advantage of high-volume standard inventory.
  • Regulators and payers: pass-through and codes favour innovators; MDR favours scale.
  • Suppliers: additive manufacturing reduces the value of precision machining, a long-run threat to Basel's advantage.
  • Hospitals as manufacturers: point-of-care printing (Rambam) is an early signal of disintermediation for custom CMF cases.
