3b

Industry structure

10 answers
Judgement60C-2456C-2555C-2662C-2762C-2862C-2956C-3158C-3554C-3664
Group judgementGroup judgement: Industry and Value Chain (Layer 1)C6015th of 36full answer under 3 Value chain dynamics and industry structure
C-24

Where does the company rank by market share or size for its activity or product, and how far is it from the number one or two position?

F5629th of 36

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement. Medartis is number one or two globally in its core niche, hand and wrist fixation (it claims over 30% global share), and a leader in thumb-base joint replacement through TOUCH (43,000 units in 2025, with no direct equivalent among the US majors). In every larger frame it is a small player: about 2% of US upper extremities, about 1.5% of the US overall, low single digits of the global trauma and extremities market where Stryker (about 25%) and DePuy Synthes (about 23%) lead. The ranking that matters for growth is the US one, where Medartis is far from the top two.

Reasoning. The >30% global hand and wrist share is a company claim (2024 investor presentation) and is driven by Europe; in the US the incumbents are Acumed (volar wrist plates), Stryker, DePuy Synthes and Skeletal Dynamics. Medartis's US sales of about CHF 50m (FY2025) set against a US upper-extremities market representing most of a global extremities market of USD 9bn to 10bn confirm a low share. In CMF it competes with Stryker, DePuy Synthes and KLS Martin in a CHF 1.5bn to 1.8bn market, with an above-average position in digital planning (CMX). This structure is the opportunity (long US runway) and the risk (Medartis is a price-taker on contracts in the US).

C-25

How concentrated is the market: do the top five hold more than 30 percent, and how steep is the share distribution (for example, do the top five hold five times the share of numbers six to ten)?

D5525th of 36

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement. Highly concentrated in the broad frame and steep. In trauma and extremities the top four hold about 64% (Stryker 25%, DePuy Synthes 23%, Zimmer Biomet 12%, Smith & Nephew 4%); in global trauma the top four hold about 70%; the top three musculoskeletal companies are 50 to 121 times larger than specialists. The top five comfortably exceed 30%, and the share of the top five is far more than five times that of numbers six to ten, which are specialists such as Medartis, Acumed, Arthrex's extremities line, Treace and Skeletal Dynamics. Within hand and wrist the niche is concentrated around a few specialists plus the majors.

Why it matters. The concentration is in the hands of large, diversified, margin-focused companies whose pricing has been rational (see C-28). That protects price levels. But it also means Medartis's US growth requires taking share from incumbents who control hospital contracts through bundling across product franchises; the contract, not the surgeon, often decides the device (expert finding, Leadership digest).

C-26

How stable are market shares over time, and what share have new entrants taken over the last three to five years?

C6213th of 36

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement. Shares among the majors are stable; the movement is at the edge, where focused specialists take share in fast-growing niches and value-tier local makers take share in emerging-market public tenders. New entrants proper have taken almost nothing in developed markets because of regulatory cost, training-based switching costs and contract lock-in.

Evidence. Medartis has grown 15% to 17% organically against a market at about 9%, so it has been gaining share at roughly 6 to 8 points a year of relative growth; Paragon 28 grew over 18% in foot and ankle, three times its market; Globus trauma grew 30% in Q1 2026 from a small base. Large players lose share slowly (Smith & Nephew's US knee share fell from 10.7% to 8.5% in 2019 to 2024 in large joints). No specialised new mortgage-like entrant has emerged in hand and wrist; the newcomers are digital and 3D-printing firms (Materialise, SINTX, CADskills before its acquisition) in patient-specific niches, with negligible share. In LATAM and Asia, low-cost local makers (Hofer, GMReis, Orthotech; Korean and Chinese makers in other categories) take public tender share, which is why Medartis bought NeoOrtho.

C-27

To what extent does market share consolidate over time towards the largest competitors, and towards the most advantaged ones (not necessarily the largest or most established), and is this company among them?

D6217th of 36

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement. Share consolidates towards the largest by acquisition (Stryker/Wright Medical USD 5.4bn; Zimmer/Paragon 28 USD 1.1bn; Acumed's roll-up of ExsoMed and TECHFIT) and towards the most advantaged by organic growth (specialists with training, product and focused sales forces). Medartis is among the advantaged organically in hand, wrist and thumb-base arthroplasty, but not among the largest, and the acquisitions of specialists by majors show that "most advantaged specialist" is often an interim state before being bought.

Reasoning. The organic share flow in extremities runs to companies whose surgeon education and dedicated channels beat the majors' bundled, multi-franchise sales forces in niche procedures (Jefferies: specialists resist large-joint bundling with dedicated technical sales forces; hand surgeons choose independently of hip and knee buyers). Medartis's 46% versus 16% growth for exclusive versus multiline distributors in the US is direct evidence of this mechanism. The counter-force is the hospital contract (90% of hospitals on volume agreements, Stryker policing compliance), which favours the largest. Medartis is therefore advantaged with the surgeon and disadvantaged with the administrator.

C-28

What is competitors' attitude to competition: do they put margin and return on capital first, or volume first; are they benign and cooperative, or willing to suffer losses; and do industry prices fall when volume falls?

C-6216th of 36

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement. The majors put margin and return first, are broadly benign on price and compete through product breadth, bundling, robotics and digital tools; low-cost local makers put volume first and compete in public tenders; Acumed (private) is the most aggressive specialist, with fixed-price surgery-centre packages. Industry prices have historically not collapsed when volume fell, in part because trauma and hand procedures are non-deferrable.

Evidence. Expert calls: the main orthopaedic companies avoided price rises during the inflationary period and kept implant pricing consistent despite hospital cost containment; multinationals prioritise margin over price-war volume (Business Foundations). Structural price erosion of about 1% to 2% a year comes from GPOs, not from competitor aggression. In 2009 large-joint volumes kept rising (hip +6%, knee +11% after the crisis, Morgan Stanley); in 2020 volumes fell but prices held. Australia's 20% to 25% cuts were government-imposed, not competitor-driven. The risk is localised: Japan's former distributor competing hard on wrist, and Acumed's ASCent bundles in surgery centres.

C-29

How stable is that attitude, and how has it changed over the last eighteen months to three years: do competitors show 'Michael O'Leary' instability (hard to predict, situation-dependent, reactive), and would they launch a 'shark attack' on industry pricing if they sensed weakness?

C-5617th of 36

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement. The attitude is stable among the majors and has become more active, not more aggressive: over 18 months they have launched competing products (Pangea, Acu-Loc NEXT, VOLT plating), bought specialists and built surgery-centre programmes. No major has shown O'Leary-style volatility. A shark attack on hand and wrist pricing is unlikely while the majors have bigger battles in large joints and robotics, but three things could destabilise it: the DePuy Synthes separation (a standalone company under pressure to show growth), Smith & Nephew under activist pressure, and Zimmer Biomet's integration of Paragon 28 creating a scaled extremities rival.

For the model. Do not model a price war. Model rising competitive cost (training, trial sets, field specialists) and modest share pressure in lower extremities and Japan.

C-31

Taken together, how supportive is the industry structure, and how fragile is that support?

D5820th of 36

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement. The structure is moderately supportive: a growing niche, rational large competitors, low supplier power, high regulatory and training barriers to entry, and stable pricing. It is fragile in two places: buyer power (GPOs, hospital consortia, tenders, administrators), which slowly erodes price and gates new products; and the possibility that a major chooses to target hand and wrist with bundling, as Stryker and Acumed have begun to do. The support is stronger in Europe (where Medartis is the leader) than in the US (where it is the challenger).

C-35To what extent are new entrants (from adjacent markets; standard entrants; orthogonal or innovative entrants) entering or threatening this market, and how much of a threat is each type?D5420th of 36full answer under 1a External headwinds/threats
C-36

To what extent are substitutes or alternatives (other ways for customers to solve the problems the company's products solve) a threat, and how is that changing?

C+649th of 36

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement. Substitution works in Medartis's favour more than against it. TOUCH is itself the substitute, replacing trapeziectomy, an established but slower-recovery procedure, so the substitution trend is a growth driver. For plates and screws, the alternatives (intramedullary nails, which Medartis now offers with Titan Nail; conservative casting; bioresorbable screws; patient-specific implants) are either already in the portfolio or slow-moving.

Watch item. If a large competitor launches its own CMC 1 implant (the Change Forces report flags "entrenched competitors could introduce competing thumb implants"), TOUCH's substitution advantage would narrow; a new entrant would need a PMA pathway (years) to reach the US.

Strong business SRCA and BTE; management; financial profile

–not yet rated

Memo topic 3, : the Rater's score

4a

Business quality

D6018th of 368 answers
Judgement60C-4162C-4240C-4376C-4468C-4550C-4645C-4758
Group judgement

Company - Competitive advantage

D6018th of 36

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

Medartis has a defensible niche franchise, not a fortress. Its advantages are the specialist's advantages: clinical differentiation, surgeon training lock-in, and a single exceptional product (TOUCH) protected by a high regulatory and reimbursement barrier. It lacks the giants' advantages of scale, contracts and bundling, and its plating design lead is being matched. The combination supports continued share gains and a premium price but not price increases above inflation, and it explains why margins are a scale question. A realistic addressable market several times its size means the growth runway is not the constraint for at least five years. For the model: share gains and mix, not price; margin improvement only as scale rises.

C-41

Which sustainable relative competitive advantages or moats does the company hold (customer-related; production-related; scale-based; regulatory or political; culture or knowledge), how strong is each, and are they widening or narrowing?

D6222nd of 36

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement. Medartis holds three real advantages and lacks the two that the giants hold. Its strongest and widening moat is the TOUCH franchise (regulatory, clinical and reimbursement barriers). Its broadest moat is surgeon lock-in through training and proprietary locking mechanics, strong in Europe and now being built in the US. Its product-design lead in plating is real but narrowing as competitors reach parity. It has no scale advantage and no contract or bundling power, which are the giants' moats.

By type.

  • Regulatory and clinical (TOUCH): strong, widening. PMA approval (the hardest US route, five years of work), ten-plus years of European clinical use, a five-year ODEP rating, a US CPT code, Medicare pass-through, Australian listing. A rival would need years and its own PMA. The franchise is now fully owned (KeriMedical 100%).
  • Customer-related (surgeon lock-in): strong in Europe, building in the US. Surgeons stay with the locking system and screw mechanics they trained on (TriLock, HexaDrive); IBRA trained 1,450 professionals in 2025; switching means retraining in an operating room where routine matters; the US build-out (330 hand surgeons targeted in 2026, M.A.R.C. Institute, Orlando) widens this in the market that matters. Exclusive distributors (46% growth against 16% for multiline) add a channel-loyalty layer.
  • Product and know-how (plating design, CMX digital planning): moderate, narrowing in plating. Stryker Pangea, Acumed Acu-Loc NEXT and Globus elbow claim parity; CMX and CADskills keep an edge in complex CMF, where Acumed/TECHFIT and Materialise compete.
  • Production: moderate. Swiss precision and 24/7 automation give quality and 81% gross margin, but at a high franc cost base; local plants (Warsaw, Curitiba, France) add flexibility, not cost leadership.
  • Scale-based: absent. 50 to 121 times smaller than the top three; Stryker reps sell about three times more per territory (Smith & Nephew source, a large-joint analogue).
  • Regulatory barrier (MDR, FDA): mild positive, raising the cost of entry for small challengers.
C-42

How large is the company's scale relative to competitors and other actors (revenue, headcount and so on), and what does that scale confer?

F4032nd of 36

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement. Medartis is subscale against every major competitor and against its buyers, and scale confers only focus, not cost advantage. With about CHF 340m of 2026 sales it is a fraction of Stryker's trauma and extremities business (about USD 1bn a quarter) or DePuy Synthes trauma (about USD 0.8bn a quarter), and smaller than Acumed's private group in hand and wrist in the US. In its European niche it has local scale: the leading hand and wrist share, a dense training network and direct sales.

What scale does and does not give. It gives a focused hand-surgeon sales force and fast decisions (the distributor reset, Project Flash). It does not give purchasing leverage, contract bundling, procurement scale or fixed-cost absorption: Medartis runs five or six plants for a CHF 300m business, R&D at about 12% of sales against 5% to 8% at the majors, and a selling cost ratio well above the mid-20% peer median. The margin gap to the majors (core EBITDA 18% against Stryker about 28% and Globus about 34%) is mostly a scale gap. Growth to CHF 500m by 2028/29 (two brokers' path) would be the main source of margin improvement, which is why operating leverage is the central modelling question.

C-43How large is the realistic total addressable market relative to the company, and what rate of growth does it allow for over the mid term?A763rd of 36full answer under 1b External tailwinds
C-44

How high is the company's surplus customer utility against competing or alternative offerings, and is it leading, or at least demonstrably not inferior? (Hard to prove, easier to disprove; the aim is to disprove inferiority at Early-Stage and Deep Dive, for example Sophos, Bytes.)

C+688th of 36

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement. Inferiority is disproved in its core products and leadership is evident in TOUCH. In hand and wrist plating Medartis is demonstrably not inferior (global share leadership, a sustained 10% to 15% price premium, share gains wherever its exclusive channel operates). In thumb-base arthroplasty it is leading (a clinically differentiated implant with faster recovery than trapeziectomy, adopted ahead of plan). The one place where its utility is being questioned is Japan, where the former distributor's offering is winning wrist cases.

Evidence. 46% growth for exclusive distributors (surgeons follow the product when the rep is dedicated); market share gains in the UK, Spain and Germany despite strikes; US organic growth 27.4%; TOUCH surgeries above plan; Australian reimbursement price near CHF 1,300 (payers accept the premium); 93% on-time-in-full delivery (service utility); compact sterile procedure kits valued by surgery centres. Counter-evidence: Japan wrist loss; experts say device choice is often decided by hospital contract, not by surgeon preference, which caps the value of utility in GPO-controlled accounts.

C-45

To what extent does the company have, and use, demonstrable pricing power with minimal erosion of its relative surplus customer utility, shown by evidence of application rather than conjecture?

D5019th of 36

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement. Pricing power is demonstrated in one place, held but not exercised in the core, and absent where buyers are consolidated. Evidence of application: TOUCH's premium price backed by reimbursement codes, the defence of a 10% to 15% premium in plating, and a 3% US tariff-related price increase (accepted on non-contract business). Evidence of limits: GPO price-downs of 1% to 2% a year, government cuts absorbed in Australia and Thailand, the tariff increases covering only a third of the tariff cost because 60% of US sales are on multi-year contracts.

For the model. Revenue growth will be overwhelmingly volume and mix; assume roughly zero like-for-like price in plating, and model gross-margin uplift from TOUCH mix (KeriMedical carries higher margin than group average) and from replacing distributed products with own products.

C-46

To what extent does the company share its scale economies with customers, stated or de facto, in its messaging, strategy, culture or values?

D4515th of 36

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement. Little, and it is not the model. Medartis is a premium-price, premium-service specialist; its customer proposition is clinical outcome, training and service rather than shared cost savings. The partial exceptions are the value tier (NeoOrtho bids low in public tenders without discounting the Medartis brand), compact procedure kits that reduce hospital sterilisation and inventory costs, and free surgeon education. These lower the customer's total cost, but they are tactics, not a stated principle of returning scale gains to customers.

C-47

To what extent do gravitational or mechanical forces, rather than heroic company dynamism, improve the company's competitive advantage and its financial outputs against peers with each period that passes?

C-5817th of 36

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement. Some gravitational forces are at work and are strengthening, but the present improvement is mostly management-driven. The mechanical forces are: the accumulating base of trained surgeons and placed consignment sets (each new account consumes implants for years); the accumulating clinical record of TOUCH; the end-market growing at 9%; the move of US production to Warsaw (each period lowers tariff and dollar-cost mismatch); and the TOUCH reimbursement architecture. The heroic forces are the distributor reset, M&A, new country entries and the US training push.

Why it matters. Gravitational forces are what make a forecast predictable. Today about half of the growth story (market growth, installed base, TOUCH clinical record) would continue under average management; the other half depends on the present team executing six programmes at once.

Medartis has a strong, fast, honest operator at its head, a coherent strategy and a leadership team of high calibre but thin depth, all assembled in the last two years. C-55

C-56

C-63

Explain, with the evidence, the extent to which the company puts shareholders first, ahead of management, and how prudent its governance is (scaffolding put in place even where it was not needed): what does the evidence show, and why?

D4624th of 36

Layer 1 · Company - Management

Judgement. Governance is mixed: good on board independence and pay design, weak on minority protection, audit and shareholder distributions. Medartis is run for the long term by an anchor owner, which is good for the business and only partly good for minority shareholders.

Evidence for. Board of eight, seven independent (the Risks report counts two non-independent under internal guidelines, Straumann and Miesch); independent chairman separate from the CEO; 37.5% women; annual say-on-pay; short-term incentive on core metrics excluding M&A items, capped at 200%; long-term plan 100% performance shares on relative TSR and cash flow; minimum shareholding introduced 2026; compliance training 92%, no fines; auditor fees modest with no non-audit work; related-party flows disclosed and modest (IBRA CHF 4.6m, associates CHF 4.7m, Straumann Group CHF 34k).

Evidence against. Founder controls 39.9% of votes with an opting-out clause that removes the mandatory bid for minorities; share transfers and nominee registrations need board approval; conditional and band capital allow up to 38.8% dilution from convertibles without pre-emptive rights; Ernst & Young auditor since 2004 (lead partner rotated 2023); no internal audit function; no dividend or buyback; fixed executive pay limits raised for LATAM expansion and benchmarking; founder and former CEO selling shares. The design is prudent for continuity; it is not "scaffolding put in place even where not needed".

C-49

What earnings-per-share growth is the company generating, with what revenue growth, and how durable is it?

D5022nd of 36

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement. EPS has not tracked revenue: over FY2023 to FY2025 revenue grew at a compound rate of about 13% while core EPS swung from about CHF 0.35 to CHF 0.92 to CHF 0.49 (diluted), and reported basic EPS from CHF 0.05 to CHF 0.29 to CHF 0.67. EPS is small, volatile and dominated by below-EBIT items (convertible interest, FX losses, a volatile tax rate). H1 2026 core EPS of CHF 0.56 against roughly zero a year earlier is the first sign of earnings catching up with sales, but about CHF 6.7m of the improvement in the financial result came from currency gains. The brokers' path (CHF 1.55, 2.50, 3.79 for FY2026 to FY2028) is a 60% a year compounding that rests on margin expansion not yet demonstrated. EPS durability is low today and potentially high later.

Why the gap. Core EBIT margin has been flat at about 9% to 9.5% (FY2024 9.5%, FY2025 9.2%, H1 2026 about 9.0%), so EBIT grows with sales; then interest (CHF 6.8m a year on the convertible from 2025), FX (CHF -5.2m in FY2025, positive in H1 2026) and tax (rates of 49.7%, -55.3% and 29.2% in FY2023 to FY2025 on FactSet's reported basis) produce the swings. Sensitivities (company-derived, Change Forces report): +1 point of sales growth adds about CHF 0.16 to annualised EPS; +50 basis points of EBIT margin adds about CHF 0.10; a 5% USD/CHF fall costs about CHF 0.08.

C-50

How much immediate scope is there to deploy capital or operating spend at a high return on new invested capital for three to five years, and what does that mean for mid- and long-term potential and the fair multiple?

C6011th of 36

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement. The scope to deploy capital is large (US share, TOUCH, LATAM value, training), but the return on new capital has so far been modest because growth is capital-hungry: each CHF 100 of extra sales needs roughly CHF 30 to 35 of working capital (sets, inventory, receivables) and CHF 10 to 15 of fixed assets. At the historical incremental EBIT margin of about 8% to 15%, that gives a post-tax return on incremental capital of roughly 13% to 25%. If the incremental EBIT margin rises to the 20% to 25% implied by the two brokers' FY2027 and FY2028 numbers, the incremental return rises to about 35%. The fair multiple therefore depends directly on whether operating leverage arrives; at today's returns the business earns its cost of capital on growth only moderately.

Evidence. FactSet net working capital rose from CHF 124m (June 2025) to CHF 146m (June 2026) on about CHF 70m more trailing sales; NWC is about 47% of trailing sales, inventory about 36%; capex at 9% to 11% of sales (FY2025 CHF 25.5m; FY2026 tracking about CHF 35m, the brokers' figure); five-year depreciation of instrument sets. Positive: the training-led model is "one-to-many" (IBRA throughput rising faster than headcount); TOUCH needs fewer sets per procedure than trauma plating. The Wisdom Library's "capacity to suffer" test applies: deteriorating cash on a deliberate investment programme is acceptable only if a leading measure of traction is visible (it is: US +27%, TOUCH ahead of plan) and a long-horizon owner absorbs the damage (it is: the founder with 39.9%). Both conditions hold, which supports paying for growth, but at a multiple that assumes the return on new capital rises.

C-51

How high are the returns on capital: post-tax return on new invested capital, on capital employed and on tangible capital?

F3034th of 36

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement. Returns are low today. On a rough calculation from the files, post-tax return on invested capital (including the goodwill from KeriMedical and NeoOrtho) is about 5%, return on capital employed about 6% to 7% pre-tax, and pre-tax return on tangible capital about 10% to 11% (about 8% post-tax). All are below a Swiss small-cap cost of capital of about 8% (DNB Carnegie's figure in the Risks report), except tangible capital at the margin. Morgan Stanley's 2019 estimate of 13% ROIC shows returns were higher before the acquisitions and the investment cycle. These figures are the analyst's approximations and should be rebuilt in Set 2.

Calculation. FY2025 core EBIT CHF 24.5m, at about 25% tax a NOPAT of about CHF 18m. Invested capital: equity about CHF 275m (49% of CHF 560m assets) plus net financial debt about CHF 80m plus leases about CHF 22m, about CHF 380m: ROIC about 5%. Tangible capital: net working capital about CHF 132m plus net fixed assets and capitalised sets of roughly CHF 90m to 100m, about CHF 225m: pre-tax return about 11%. Return on new invested capital, see C-50 (13% to 25% historically, about 35% if the brokers' margins arrive). The long-term plan's cash-flow metric and the new finance director's cash agenda target exactly this weakness.

C-52

How high is the conversion of net income into free cash flow, and how do net working capital and similar items behave?

F2834th of 36

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement. Cash conversion is poor and was negative in H1 2026; working capital is the main reason and will keep absorbing cash while the company grows at high teens. The headline FactSet conversion ratios (free cash flow at 148% to 812% of net income) are meaningless because net income is tiny. The relevant measures: free cash flow (after intangible capex) of CHF 21.0m (FY2024), CHF 9.3m (FY2025) and CHF -10.1m (H1 2026); FCF as a share of core EBITDA about 50%, 19% and -35%.

Working capital behaviour. NWC absorbed CHF 11.5m in FY2024, released CHF 7.3m in FY2025 (inventories down CHF 5.7m, payables up CHF 6.3m, the latter partly stretching from CHF 8.9m to CHF 16.1m) and absorbed CHF 25.0m in H1 2026 (inventory for new sets CHF 4.1m, the CHF 4.4m tariff refund receivable, and the reversal of the payables stretch). Receivable days held at 59. Inventory at CHF 98m (36% of sales) is in the bottom 5% globally for capital intensity; total set value CHF 64.8m. The H1 is seasonally weaker (H2 2025 cash inflow was driven by lower receivable days), so FY2026 FCF should be around zero rather than strongly negative, consistent with the two brokers' CHF 3.4m. The CHF 4.4m tariff refund, once collected, is a one-off inflow.

For the model. Model NWC at 30% to 35% of incremental sales, capex at 10% of sales in 2026 easing to 6% to 8% by 2028 as the plant programme ends, and FCF only turning meaningfully positive in FY2028. That leaves no internal funding for further acquisitions.

C-53

How high are gross and operating profit margins, and how are they trending?

D5521st of 36

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement. Gross margin is among the highest in orthopaedics (core 81% to 82%, against 65% to 70% at Stryker, Globus and Integra) and is recovering; operating margin is low and flat. The 60-point gap between gross and EBIT margin is the cost of a specialist, training-intensive, consignment-based model run at small scale.

Trend.

  • Core gross margin: about 83% before 2024 (83.3% excluding contract manufacturing in H1 2024), 82.8% FY2024, 81.0% FY2025 (-0.9 points tariffs, -0.6 currency, plus capacity built ahead of demand and NeoOrtho dilution), 81.5% H1 2026 (+0.8 points despite 1.2 points of tariff and currency drag, thanks to TOUCH and the KeriMedical margin). Reported IFRS gross margin is lower (75.9% FY2025) because of the inventory step-up and NeoOrtho.
  • Core EBITDA margin: 17.1% (FY2023), 19.0% (FY2024), 18.4% (FY2025), 18.0% (H1 2026); guided "high teens" at constant currency.
  • Core EBIT margin: 9.5% (FY2024), 9.2% (FY2025; +1.2 points at constant currency), about 9.0% (H1 2026).
  • The 9-point gap between EBITDA and EBIT is unusually wide: depreciation and amortisation are about 9.5% of sales, much of it on instrument sets and capitalised development, which are really operating costs of growth. Treat EBIT, not EBITDA, as the honest profit measure.

Outlook. Gross margin has a mechanical path back towards 82% to 83% by 2027/28 (tariffs fall, Warsaw scale, TOUCH mix, Titan Nail), offset by NeoOrtho and currency. Operating margin expansion depends on selling and R&D leverage (C-75, C-78).

C-54

How clean is the earnings base: how large is the gap between adjusted and reported earnings; do one-offs, capitalised costs or other items flatter it; and does the tax rate or any other non-obvious leakage erode earnings per share?

F3428th of 36

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

The Investment Analyst's heading How clean is the earnings base: how large is the gap between adjusted and reported earnings; do one-offs, capitalised costs or other items flatter it; and does the tax rate or any other non-obvious leakage erode EPS?

Judgement. The earnings base is not clean. The core-to-IFRS reconciliation is long and changes every period; development capitalisation is rising faster than sales and has been written off once before; currency gains and losses move net profit by amounts similar to net profit itself; the tax rate is erratic; and the convertible adds a non-cash accretion charge. Core earnings are a reasonable operating measure, but EPS forecasts built on them carry wide error bands.

  • Tax: core tax expense rose to CHF 5.2m in FY2025 on transfer-pricing adjustments; reported rates have swung from -55% to +70% over seven years; a normalised 22% to 26% is a modelling assumption, not an observed rate.

  • Minorities: 49% of NeoOrtho's profit belongs to minorities until the 2028/29 buy-out (small).
C-76

How material is the financial leverage or gearing: how much does it amplify earnings per share and risk, and how comfortable is it?

D4627th of 36

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

The Investment Analyst's heading How material is the financial leverage or gearing: how much does it amplify EPS and risk, and how comfortable is it?

Judgement. Gearing is moderate in leverage ratios and significant in its effect on EPS. Net financial debt excluding leases is about CHF 84m (convertible CHF 98.1m carrying plus bank CHF 12m less cash CHF 25.8m), about 1.5x trailing core EBITDA of about CHF 56m; including leases and the NeoOrtho put, about 2.2x; on FactSet's all-in total debt (CHF 207m) about 3.2x. The equity ratio fell from 59% to 49% in a year. Any further acquisition of size would likely need equity.

Pricing power and operating leverage (and others)

5a

Pricing power

3 answers
C-1748C-4550P-0450
C-17To what extent is the company raising prices in real terms (above cost inflation), and are the rises real, merely nominal, or so far only latent or plausible and increasingly noted by management?D4820th of 36full answer under 2b Internal corporate change forces
C-45To what extent does the company have, and use, demonstrable pricing power with minimal erosion of its relative surplus customer utility, shown by evidence of application rather than conjecture?D5019th of 36full answer under 4a Business quality
P-04

After assessing the pricing power in greater detail, how is price likely to contribute to revenue and margin over the next 18 months to four years?

C508th of 23

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement. Like-for-like price will contribute roughly nothing to slightly negative (-0.5% to 0% a year), and mix will contribute +1 to +2 points a year to revenue growth from 2026 to 2028, almost entirely from US TOUCH and secondarily from own products replacing distributed ones. Price and mix together therefore add about 1 to 1.5 points a year to revenue growth and about 0.3 to 0.5 points a year to gross margin until 2028, fading after. Medartis's pricing power is real in one product in one country (TOUCH in the US) and absent in the contracted and tendered plating business that is about three-quarters of sales. The largest pricing risk in the forecast window is the end of TOUCH's US transitional pass-through payment, which began on 1 January 2026 and typically lasts two to three years (expiry at end-2027 or end-2028). I model a 5% fall in the US TOUCH selling price in each of 2028 and 2029 (about CHF 2.5m to 3m of revenue a year, about 0.5 points of group growth) and treat a 15% to 25% cut as the downside (CHF 8m to 12m of revenue and 0.8 to 1.0 points of EBITDA margin in 2028-2029).

Reasoning and evidence.

Plating: price-taker, offset by productivity. About 62% of US sales are under formal GPO or hospital-group contracts with automatic price-downs of 1% to 3% a year for standard trauma plating (company, AlphaSense pricing report); the selective US price rise of about 3% in early 2026 could apply only outside multi-year contracts and was meant to fund about a third of the tariff cost. Australia's cuts of 12%, 5% and 5% (2023 to July 2024, fully annualised by mid-2025) left APAC revenue flat while volumes grew in the mid-teens. Swiss and German hospitals are pressing for concessions (German hybrid DRGs; Swiss reimbursement declining). The claimed 10% to 15% premium over generalists is not visible in any contract or tender data. Against that, core gross margin has stayed at 81% to 83% for years, so manufacturing productivity has historically offset the price-downs; I assume it continues to do so, which is why I model gross margin rising only through mix and absorption.

TOUCH: the one product with pricing power, and the most concentrated revenue driver in the model. Management cited a stable US average selling price of about USD 6,500 (August 2026), against Australian Prescribed List reimbursement of about CHF 1,300 from July 2026; the US unit is worth roughly four times an ex-US unit. At about 3,000 US procedures in 2026 that is about CHF 15m to 16m of revenue (about 4.5% of group sales); at 7,000 in 2027 about CHF 37m. The US TOUCH increase alone is about six points of my 2027 group growth of 16.5%, and about three to four points in 2028. Its gross margin is above the group average (the company calls the KeriMedical business accretive; core gross margin rose 0.8 points in H1 2026 despite 1.2 points of tariff and currency drag). TOUCH holds over 70% share in well-penetrated European markets and 85% in France, and no competitor has a US-approved equivalent; a rival needs a premarket approval, years away.

The pass-through question. Pass-through pays the hospital for the device above the standard outpatient rate; when it ends, the device cost is packaged into the procedure payment, and the hospital's margin on the procedure, and so its tolerance of the price, depends on the payment CMS then sets. This is not necessarily a cliff: Bonesupport bridged a similar transition with a favourable DRG reassignment from 1 October 2026. But the hospital administrator, not the surgeon, is the 2026 gate (Bonesupport's Q2 2026 stall; value analysis committees taking up to four months). My base case assumes a modest price give-back; the evidence on the post-pass-through payment is the highest-value research gap in the pricing question (a research request asks for it).

Value tier and mix dilution. NeoOrtho's tender pricing in Brazil (LATAM gross margin 70% in FY2025 against 78% to 87% elsewhere) dilutes mix as LATAM grows; I hold LATAM at about 9% of sales, so the dilution is small (about -0.1 points of group gross margin a year).

What it means for each line. Revenue: price -0.5% to 0%, mix +1 to +2 points (2026-28), then +0.5 to +1. Gross margin: +0.3 to +0.5 points a year to 2028 from mix, offset partly by price-downs on plating; I land at 82.3% (2027), 82.6% (2028), 83.0% (2030). EBITDA: pricing adds nothing beyond its gross-margin effect, because the selling cost of placing TOUCH (field experts, training) scales with it.

5b

Operating leverage

2 answers
C-7560P-0158
C-75

How material is the fixed-cost operating leverage: how much does a given change in revenue move operating profit, and how wide a range of outcomes does that create?

B+604th of 36

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement. Theoretical operating leverage is high, realised leverage has been low, and the range of outcomes is wide. With an 81% gross margin and a largely fixed cost base (R&D, administration, plants, a salaried training and sales infrastructure), each extra franc of sales should drop through at 40% to 60% at EBIT if costs were held; in practice the incremental core EBITDA margin was about 15% in FY2025 and about 19% in H1 2026 because management reinvested and because currency and tariffs intervened.

Range. Variable costs are roughly cost of goods (19%) plus distributor commissions and variable selling (perhaps 10% to 15% of sales in indirect US markets; not disclosed), so contribution margin is about 65% to 70%. If FY2027 sales rise by CHF 70m and fixed costs rise only 10%, EBIT margin could jump 4 to 5 points; if fixed costs rise in line with sales (as in FY2025), margin stays flat. On the downside, a 5-point shortfall in growth (about CHF 17m of sales) would cut EBIT by CHF 6m to 9m, a 15% to 25% EPS hit, because the cost base for 17% to 19% growth (capacity at Besançon, Warsaw, Curitiba; US training; LATAM entries) is already committed. The Risks report's Failure #5 (manufacturing underutilisation) is this mechanism.

For the model. Build the cost base bottom-up (fixed versus variable by line) and test three settings: reinvest-all (incremental EBITDA margin about 18%), partial release (about 25%, roughly the brokers' path) and full release (about 35% to 40%). The FY2027 guidance on 2 March 2027 is the best evidence of which one management intends.

P-01

After assessing the fixed-cost operating leverage in greater detail, how is it likely to shape the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years, against the last four to six years?

B+583rd of 23

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement. Operating leverage will finally show, but muted and back-loaded, and it will show more in EBIT than in EBITDA. I expect the core EBITDA margin (on IFRS sales) to rise from 18.1% in FY2025 to 18.6% in FY2026, 19.5% in FY2027 and about 22.2% by FY2030, roughly 90 basis points a year, and the core EBIT margin faster, from 9.1% to about 14.2% (about 125 basis points a year), because depreciation falls as a share of sales once the 2025-2027 plant and instrument-set programme peaks. That is a break from FY2019 to FY2025, when revenue doubled but the margin rose only about three points and never held above 19%, and core EBIT stayed near 9%. Two features of the leverage matter more than its average rate:

  1. It is asymmetric. Upside revenue is largely reinvested (the "high teens at constant currency" guidance habit and the US build-out absorb it, so drop-through on a beat is about 20%), while a revenue shortfall drops through at about 50% to 60%, because the cost base for 17% to 19% growth (six plants, US education centres, field experts, LATAM entries) is already committed. Margin risk is therefore skewed down even though revenue risk in the next 18 months is skewed up.
  2. The decisive variable is revenue, not management's chosen incremental margin. My incremental EBITDA margins (about 25% in FY2027, 28% in FY2028 and 29% in FY2029 to FY2030) are close to the two brokers' (25.5% and 27.8% for FY2027 and FY2028). My FY2027 and FY2028 EBITDA sit 8% to 11% below consensus because my revenue sits 5% to 8% below it, not because I assume less leverage.

Reasoning and evidence.

The cost structure. With an 81.5% core gross margin (H1 2026), variable cost of goods is about 18.5% of sales. Distributor commissions are variable in indirect markets (independent US agents typically earn 30% to 45% of sales, expert call, AlphaSense feedback-loops report), and logistics (9.8% of sales in 2025 against 9.5% in 2024) moves with volume; selling and distribution was 37.1% of sales in H1 2026 against 36.4% in H1 2025, so it rose, not fell. Personnel cost was CHF 127.6m in 2025 (about 47% of sales) and is mostly fixed in the short run; Warsaw ran below 20% of capacity in March 2025; six plants serve a CHF 340m business. My estimate of the contribution margin is about 60% to 65%, which is why theoretical leverage is high.

The long-run record is the right anchor, not the last 18 months. Core EBITDA rose from about CHF 19.5m (FY2019, a 15% margin on CHF 130m) to CHF 48.8m (FY2025) while core sales rose by about CHF 136m: an average incremental margin of about 21.5% over six years that include Covid, the 2023 ransomware halt, the 2025 tariff shock and franc strength. By period: FY2021 about 25% (margin +1.7 points on +25% growth), FY2024 well above average (19.0% margin on 11.7% growth, from tight headquarters cost control), FY2025 about 15% and H1 2026 about 19% (currency, tariffs, capacity ahead of demand, NeoOrtho dilution and a mid-single-digit CHF million US investment in ten TOUCH field experts absorbed it). The low recent figures are the exception produced by specific absorbers, most of which are fading, rather than the rule.

What moves FY2027 (from 18.6% to 19.5%, +0.9 points). Tariff cost falling (+0.3 to +0.5 points), TOUCH mix in gross margin (+0.3 to +0.5), underlying scale on selling and administration (+0.5 to +0.8), less a modest franc drag (about -0.3, reported basis) and a full year of US education cost (M.A.R.C. Institute in Miami, the Orlando centre) plus a permanent US President hire (-0.3 to -0.5). Cold Fusion synergies and Titan Nail add little before 2028 (P-03).

What moves FY2028 to FY2030 (to about 22.2%). The selling and distribution ratio falls from about 37% to about 34% as US territories mature (US sales roughly double from 2026 to 2028 on the same channel infrastructure), gross margin edges up to about 83% (TOUCH, own products, Warsaw absorption, less NeoOrtho), and the depreciation ratio falls from 8.7% (FY2026) to about 8.0% (FY2030) as capital spending falls from 10.6% to about 7.3% of sales. Operating cost excluding depreciation falls from 63.2% of sales (FY2026) to 60.8% (FY2030), about 60 basis points a year, close to the 70 basis points achieved between FY2024 and H1 2026 (core opex 73.8% to 72.4% of sales).

Line by line against the past.

Line FY2019-FY2025 FY2026-FY2030 (my base)
Core gross margin 81% to 83% (dips with tariffs, currency, NeoOrtho) 81.8% rising to 83.0%
Core EBITDA margin 15% to 19%, never held above 19% 18.6% rising to 22.2%; above 19% from FY2027
D&A as a share of sales rising, about 9% to 9.5% 8.7% falling to 8.0%
Core EBIT margin about 9% to 9.5% (FY2024-25) 9.9% rising to 14.2%

Sensitivity, which the Consumer will need for the cone. In FY2027 one point of organic growth is about CHF 3.4m of revenue; it is worth about CHF 0.8m of EBITDA on the upside (reinvested) and about CHF 1.9m on the downside. A five-point growth shortfall in FY2027 (about CHF 17m of revenue) would cost CHF 8m to 9m of EBITDA, taking the margin to about 18.1% (instead of 19.5%), EBIT down about 20% and EPS down about 30%. This is why EPS, not revenue, carries the widest cone.

Peers. Medacta runs a 27% to 29% adjusted EBITDA margin at EUR 600m to 700m of sales with the same franc cost base and consignment model; Straumann lifted its 2026 constant-currency EBIT margin target to +140 to +170 basis points through manufacturing scale (Shanghai) rather than cost cuts (Bernstein, June 2026); Enovis added 1.2 points of EBITDA margin in Q3 2026 from synergies. Against them, specialists rarely get selling costs below the mid-30s (Stryker's SG&A was 35.9% in Q1 2025; expert calls describe hand and extremities rep support as a fixed barrier), and Paragon 28 kept operating margins constrained at about USD 250m of sales during its rep expansion. My 22% EBITDA margin at CHF 570m in FY2030 is therefore below Medacta at similar scale: the leverage case has room above my base if management chooses to release it, which is the main upside to my margin path.

What it means for the model. Model leverage through revenue, gross margin and the selling ratio, and treat EBIT, not EBITDA, as the measure where leverage is clearest after 2027. Carry an explicit downside drop-through of about 50% on revenue shortfalls. The FY2027 guidance on 2 March 2027 is the evidence of whether management intends my path (about 19.5% in FY2027) or the brokers' (20.1%); a guide that keeps "high teens" would fit my path, a guide of "around 20%" would fit theirs.

Financial leverage

C-76How material is the financial leverage or gearing: how much does it amplify earnings per share and risk, and how comfortable is it?D4627th of 36full answer under 4c Financial profile
P-02

After assessing the financial leverage in greater detail, how is it likely to shape earnings per share and the balance sheet over the next 18 months to four years?

F4618th of 23

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement. Financial leverage is moderate on the balance sheet but a material amplifier of EPS growth. A near-fixed financing cost of about CHF 10m to 11m a year (the convertible about CHF 7m, of which about CHF 3.4m is non-cash accretion; bank borrowing about CHF 1m to 1.5m; lease interest about CHF 1.2m) falls from about 27% of core EBIT in FY2026 (before currency) to about 13% in FY2030, which adds about three points a year to EPS growth over EBIT growth. On the balance sheet, net financial debt (excluding leases and the NeoOrtho put) rises from CHF 75.5m (end-2025) to about CHF 117m (end-2026) and stays at CHF 124m to 128m through FY2029, because free cash flow after leases is negative in FY2026-27 and only modestly positive in FY2028-29 while the NeoOrtho buy-out falls in FY2029. Leverage nonetheless falls, from about 1.9 times core EBITDA (end-2026) to about 0.8 times (end-2030), because EBITDA doubles. The balance sheet can carry the organic plan without new equity, but it leaves no room for a sizeable acquisition before about 2028 without equity or new debt. The convertible is the swing item, and it affects the balance sheet and share count far more than EPS.

Reasoning and evidence.

Debt and liquidity. At June 2026: convertible CHF 98.1m carrying value (CHF 115.8m nominal, 3.0% coupon, due April 2031), bank drawings about CHF 32m (see the Preface), cash CHF 25.8m, leases of about CHF 50m (lease commitments of CHF 7.6m for machinery and CHF 41.2m for buildings at mid-2026), and the NeoOrtho liability of CHF 18.4m. FactSet's all-in net debt of CHF 181m includes leases and the put; on that basis leverage is about 3.2 times trailing core EBITDA today and about 2.9 times at end-2026. Committed bank lines are CHF 40m from two banks, and management secured a further CHF 50m of headroom; covenants are not disclosed. My path draws about CHF 37m by end-2026 and CHF 45m to 50m in 2027, leaving CHF 40m to 55m of headroom. Cash is thin (about CHF 20m at end-2026 in my base) but liquidity is adequate.

How financial leverage shapes EPS.

FY2026 FY2027 FY2028 FY2029 FY2030 CAGR 2026-30
Core EBIT 33.5 42.9 54.1 67.2 81.4 25%
Financing cost excl. currency 9.0 10.0 10.3 10.8 10.5 flat
as a share of EBIT 27% 23% 19% 16% 13%
Core EPS 1.31 1.76 2.48 3.35 4.33 35%

Of the roughly 35% EPS growth a year, about 25 points come from EBIT, about 3 points from the fixed financing cost, about 6 points from the tax rate normalising (P-08) and about half a point from the NeoOrtho minority disappearing. More than a quarter of EPS growth therefore comes from below EBIT, and the largest piece of that (tax) is the least proven.

The convertible. At CHF 109 the bond's conversion value (1.15m shares, about CHF 125m) exceeds its CHF 115.8m nominal. Holders gain nothing by converting early (they would give up the coupon), so conversion before maturity happens only if the company calls it, which it may from 2 May 2029 if the share price holds at or above CHF 130.94; at that point the call forces conversion. My FY2030 EPS of about CHF 4.3 would need a forward P/E of about 30 times (the stock's own 4th percentile today) to reach that price, so I put the call at roughly even odds. I have not put conversion into the forecast inputs, because the model has no line that would also remove the debt; the Consumer should know its effects:

  • EPS: FY2030 about CHF 4.37 if converted against CHF 4.33 if not (removing about CHF 7m of cost roughly offsets 1.15m extra shares, about 9% of the basic count): neutral to 1% accretive.
  • Balance sheet: net financial debt falls by about CHF 112m, to near zero (excluding leases) by end-2030; the equity ratio rises from about 49% towards 60%.
  • If not called, the CHF 115.8m falls due in April 2031; at about 0.8 times net debt to EBITDA in 2030 it is comfortably refinanceable.

What could break it. A further acquisition of size (management's appetite is high: KeriMedical, NeoOrtho, CADskills and M.A.R.C. in 18 months), a growth stall that keeps free cash flow negative into 2028 (P-01's 50% decremental margin plus committed capex), or a large currency loss on intercompany loans. Any of these would push all-in leverage back above three times and raise the probability of an equity raise; conditional and band capital allow it without pre-emptive rights (up to 38.8% from convertibles; a capital band to 17.86m shares authorised until April 2028).

C-77

How much of each major part of the cost base is cuttable or reducible?

C5810th of 36

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement. A meaningful share is reducible, but most of it is growth investment rather than waste, so cutting it would trade growth for margin. The truly discretionary pools are selling and marketing expansion, US training spend, LATAM entry costs and some R&D the structural costs (plants, regulatory, core R&D, set capital) are not cuttable without damaging the franchise.

By line (FY2025 approximations).

  • Cost of goods (19% of sales): partly reducible over time (tariff removal about CHF 3m to 4m; Warsaw and Curitiba automation; Titan Nail replacing NX; procurement), not cuttable in the short run.
  • Selling and distribution (about 37.7% of sales, about CHF 100m): the largest pool. Commissions are variable. Discretionary growth spend (new territories, events, field specialists) is perhaps a fifth; the UK subsidiary is already deferring non-essential events. Scale leverage, not cuts, is the main lever.
  • R&D (about 12% of sales including capitalised development): cuttable by 2 to 3 points to peer norms at a cost to the pipeline; the Risks report warns against margin built on R&D starvation.
  • General and administration: Cold Fusion synergies from 2027 (amount not disclosed); procurement; finance and IT consolidation across three brands.
  • Manufacturing overhead at five or six plants: largely fixed for three to five years; this is the main inflexible cost if growth slows.

For the model. Management could lift EBITDA margin by 3 to 5 points within two years by holding discretionary growth spend flat, which is the latent upside; it has chosen not to so far.

P-03

After assessing the scope for cost elimination and efficiency in greater detail, how much is likely to reach the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years, and when?

C+556th of 23

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement. The identifiable efficiency programme is real but small and back-loaded: about CHF 5m to 8m a year by FY2028 (1.1% to 1.8% of FY2028 sales), of which I expect about half to reach the EBITDA margin (about +0.5 to +0.8 points by FY2028) and half to be reinvested in the US commercial build-out. The larger "efficiency" is in cash, not in the income statement: capital spending falling from about 10.6% of sales (FY2026) to about 7.3% (FY2030) lifts free cash flow by about CHF 12m a year by 2030 against constant intensity and slows depreciation growth. Large cuts are feasible as a defensive option, not part of the plan: holding discretionary growth spend flat could add three to five points of EBITDA margin within two years if growth stalled, but the six-plant manufacturing overhead, the main inflexible cost, cannot be cut quickly, so cuts would cushion rather than prevent a margin fall in a downturn.

Reasoning and evidence, item by item, with timing.

Item What is known My estimate and timing Line affected
Tariff avoidance (Project Flash) 70% of US volume made in Warsaw by end-2026, about 80% by mid-2027; H1 2026 core tariff charge CHF 0.9m after refunds; savings lag 6 to 10 months through inventory CHF 1m to 2m lower cost in 2027 than in 2026; mostly in H1 2027 Gross margin, +0.3 to +0.5 points
Warsaw absorption Below 20% utilisation in March 2025; machines moved from Basel from November 2025 CHF 0.5m to 1.5m a year from 2027, rising with US volume; also a natural dollar cost hedge Gross margin
Cold Fusion (LATAM back offices merged in Curitiba, completed H1 2026) Synergies "from 2027"; no amount disclosed CHF 0.5m to 1.5m from 2027 (assumption) Operating cost; LATAM margin
Titan Nail replacing the third-party NX nail (agreement expires 2027) Launched August 2026, phased to NX volumes CHF 1m to 2m of gross profit by 2028; transition risk in 2027 Gross margin, lower extremities

Why only half reaches the margin. The company's revealed behaviour for two years is to reinvest efficiency (selling and distribution rose to 37.1% of sales in H1 2026 even as gross margin rose), and the pay scheme rewards sales growth at 50% weight against opex ratio at 30% and EBITDA margin at 20%. A finance director with a cash mandate (Dr Hackel) and a CEO who says the group is "no longer solely focusing on growth" make some release likely, not full release.

Precedents for what is cuttable. Peers show general and administrative costs and manufacturing footprints are cuttable (Smith & Nephew's 12-point plan reached USD 210m of gross run-rate savings by end-2024 and closed four plants; Straumann cut CHF 90m of operating cost in 2013 and held its EBIT margin at 18.2% by cutting back-office and marketing support only), while direct selling, training and commissions are rigid (Treace cut USD 8.2m of direct-to-consumer advertising but not surgeon training). Medartis's own 2020 precedent (EBITDA margin from 15% to 16% on a 1.9% sales fall, with short-time work subsidies) shows it can cut quickly in a crisis. Its largest cost line, selling and distribution, is the least cuttable, and the US exclusive-distributor and field-expert model raises, rather than lowers, its fixed share.

Contribution to the margin path. Of the 3.6-point EBITDA margin rise from FY2026 to FY2030 in my base, I attribute about 0.6 to 0.8 points to the efficiency items above, about 1.2 points to gross-margin mix and absorption, and about 1.7 points to scale leverage on selling and administration.

5e

Reinforcing feedback loops

2 answers
C-4758P-0560
C-47To what extent do gravitational or mechanical forces, rather than heroic company dynamism, improve the company's competitive advantage and its financial outputs against peers with each period that passes?C-5817th of 36full answer under 4a Business quality
P-05

After assessing in greater detail whether reinforcing or balancing feedback loops are at work in the business model, such that dynamics at play today create additional scope for further positive/negative dynamics in the near/mid-term (reinforcing feedback loops), or the opposite (balancing loops), how are they likely to shape the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years?

B+604th of 23

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

The Investment Analyst's heading After assessing in greater detail whether reinforcing or balancing feedback loops are at work in the business model, how are they likely to shape the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years?

Judgement. A reinforcing loop drives US revenue and is operating now on dated evidence; balancing loops govern cash and the speed of margin expansion. The net effect on the forecasts is: revenue growth stays in the mid-to-high teens through 2028 (the reinforcing loop has a 12- to 24-month lag, so the 2026 training cohort pays in 2027-28), margins expand at about 90 basis points a year rather than faster, and free cash flow turns positive only from 2028. One loop now works in the company's favour on both revenue and cost: US localisation in Warsaw.

The loops, with evidence and their financial effect.

Reinforcing loop 1: US training, TOUCH entry, cross-sell and exclusive distributors (operating now). Training (more than 180 US hand surgeons in H1 2026 against a 330 target; the M.A.R.C. Institute in Miami, 36 stations and 140 participants, acquired 17 September 2026; Orlando from Q4 2026) creates TOUCH users; TOUCH accounts open the door to APTUS hand plating (cross-selling "still building" in H1 2026, company); higher sales per account make exclusive distributors earn more and stay (exclusive partners +46% against +16% for multiline); more distributor coverage brings more surgeons to training. Evidence: US organic growth 27.4% in H1 2026; TOUCH US guidance raised from 1,200 to 1,800 to about 3,000 for 2026 within ten months. Lag: Medacta's education model converts about 70% of trained surgeons into active customers within 24 months, and 84% of its sales come from surgeons with more than three years' tenure (Morgan Stanley); the loop is slow but sticky. Financial effect: it supports US (North America) revenue growth of about 37% in 2027, 28% in 2028 and about 20% in 2029, lifting the US from 19% of group sales in 2025 to about 27% by 2028. It is the loop that gives revenue in 2027-28 more predictability than a single-product ramp would normally have.

Reinforcing loop 2: Warsaw localisation (operating, mechanical, high confidence). More US volume made locally raises Warsaw utilisation (below 20% in March 2025), lowers unit cost, removes tariff and adds a dollar cost base that offsets the unhedged dollar revenue (the former CEO called it a natural hedge in March 2024). Financial effect: +0.3 to +0.8 points of gross margin over 2027-2029 and a smaller currency sensitivity in US margins.

Balancing loop 1: sets and working capital consume the cash that growth needs (operating now, the strongest). Each new account needs consigned instrument sets and inventory (set value CHF 64.8m at June 2026; inventory about 36% of sales; working capital up CHF 25.0m in H1 2026). Each CHF 100 of extra sales needs about CHF 33 of net working capital and, in 2026-27, CHF 60 to 70 of capital spending. Financial effect: free cash flow after leases is CHF -19m (2026) and CHF -4m (2027) despite EBITDA of CHF 63m to 77m; net debt rises to about CHF 127m; acquisitions must wait or be funded externally. Medacta shows the same loop kept its free cash flow volatile for years and the market tolerated it only while growth was high.

Balancing loop 2: the guidance architecture converts revenue upside into reinvestment. Management guides margin at "high teens at constant currency" every year, raises only the sales range, and spends beats on US growth. Financial effect: caps the margin's upside response to revenue beats (about 20% drop-through) and is why my margin path is 0.6 to 0.8 points below the brokers' in FY2027-28 despite similar incremental-margin assumptions on the base case.

Balancing loop 3: committed fixed cost turns a revenue shortfall into a margin fall. Six plants, US education centres, field experts and LATAM entries were built for 17% to 19% growth. Financial effect: a downside decremental EBITDA margin of about 50% to 60% (P-01); this loop is dormant while growth holds, and becomes dominant if it does not.

Balancing loop 4: buyer power rises with share. As Medartis becomes visible in US accounts, administrators and value analysis committees respond (up to four months per hospital, local reviews up to a year; Bonesupport's "go-stop-go" in 2026; GPO bundling by the majors). Financial effect: occasional half-year pauses in US growth without loss of demand, the kind of event that widens a semi-annual reporter's cone; it argues against extrapolating any single strong half.

Balancing loop 5: the franc. Franc strength lowers reported sales and margins (FY2025: -4.4 points of growth, -1.2 points of EBITDA margin) and produces currency losses on more than CHF 200m of intercompany loans, with no hedging. Financial effect: reported EPS can swing by CHF 0.3 to 0.5 a year from currency alone in 2026-27; there is no internal loop that corrects it except slowly through Warsaw.

Which dominates, and when. Through 2028 the reinforcing US loop dominates revenue and the working-capital loop dominates cash. From 2028 the balancing cash loop weakens (capex peaks, the working-capital ratio falls from about 44% towards 41% of sales), so free cash flow and returns improve faster than revenue: my free cash flow rises from CHF 8m (2028) to CHF 31m (2030). The single event that could turn the reinforcing loop into a balancing one is a US channel or leadership failure (the US President role has been held ad interim by the finance director since H1 2026; distributor churn is undisclosed); the event that could strengthen it beyond my base is TOUCH volumes compounding faster than 7,000 units in 2027.

C-68

To what extent is company guidance above or below sell-side consensus for this financial year and next financial year, and why?

C-5513th of 36

Layer 1 · Guidance

Judgement. For FY2026 the two brokers sit at or slightly above the top of formal guidance, consistent with management's 2 November signal that it will exceed its forecast; on revenue the gap is small and plausible, on EBITDA margin consensus is a little more demanding than "high teens". For FY2027 there is no guidance (it comes on 2 March 2027), and consensus is demanding on both growth and margin relative to anything the company has said.

FY2026 arithmetic (company core figures for H1; brokers' basis for the year, so treat as approximate).

  • Revenue: consensus CHF 342m implies H2 2026 of about CHF 181m, +24% reported on H2 2025's CHF 146m. A build of organic growth of 19% to 21% (KeriMedical and TOUCH enter the organic base from July; easy US comparison after the 2025 Florida disruption), plus about 2 points from CADskills and M.A.R.C., less 2 to 4 points of currency, gives +17% to +21%, about CHF 171m to 177m, and a year of about CHF 332m to 338m. Consensus is 1% to 3% above that build; it requires H2 organic growth above 20% or a smaller currency drag. Given the 2 November statement, this is achievable.
  • EBITDA: CHF 64.8m (19.0%) implies about CHF 36m in H2 against CHF 28.8m in H1, an H2 margin near 20% against about 18.8% in H2 2025. Tariff cost was front-loaded into H1, so some H2 improvement is mechanical; but guidance is "high teens at constant currency" and the currency drag is 90 to 100 basis points. Consensus is at the upper end of what guidance allows.
  • EPS: CHF 1.55 needs H2 core net profit of about CHF 11.5m to 12m, i.e. an H2 core EBIT margin of about 10.5% to 11% and no currency loss in the financial result (H1 had about CHF 6.7m of improvement from FX gains).

FY2027. Consensus revenue CHF 416m (+21.6%), EBITDA margin 20.1%, EPS CHF 2.50 (+61%). Management's only forward markers are the US ambition (above 20%), "growth with profitability" and a habit of "high teens" margins. Consensus therefore assumes organic growth near 20% again in a year when comparisons harden (C-06), and margin release that management has not yet guided.

Why the difference. The brokers are extrapolating the 2026 acceleration and the tariff roll-off; management guides the top line conservatively and holds margin guidance flat to preserve reinvestment. The first FY2027 guide is the event that reconciles them.

C-71

How conservative is current guidance for the next one to three years against the last one to five years, adjusted for the difference in input change forces versus the past?

B+665th of 36

Layer 1 · Guidance

Judgement. FY2026 guidance is conservative once the mechanical H2 tailwinds are counted (KeriMedical entering organic, the Florida comparison, Australian reimbursement, surgeon training scale-up); the November statement confirms it. On margin, "high teens" is conservative against the input forces now in place (tariff cost falling by about CHF 3m in 2027, TOUCH mix, capex peaking), which mechanically support 50 to 150 basis points of improvement before any opex leverage. Beyond 2026 there is no formal guidance, so conservatism cannot be judged; the company's informal ambition (US above 20%) is aggressive relative to its own five-year record but consistent with the present input forces.

Comparison with the past. FY2020 to FY2025 reported growth averaged about 16% a year with core EBITDA margins of 15% to 19%; guidance for 2026 (17% to 19% organic, high-teens margin) asks for growth above the five-year average, but the input forces (owned TOUCH, US channel, reimbursement) are stronger than in any previous period, and margin guidance is at the top of the historical range rather than above it.

C-73

What else is material or uncommon about the company's guidance or investor relations?

C+5212th of 36

Layer 1 · Guidance

Judgement. Five uncommon features matter for the model and for how the market will read results.

  1. Semi-annual reporting only, with ad hoc trading statements; only two hard data points a year, so a disappointment arrives as a large surprise.
  2. Organic growth definition. Acquired businesses enter the organic base after twelve months; KeriMedical (consolidated July 2025) therefore lifts organic growth from H2 2026. Some of the acceleration in reported organic growth is a definitional effect of moving a fast-growing acquired product into the base, not an acceleration of the legacy business.
  3. Core versus IFRS. The core bridge is long; broker "adjusted" figures differ from company core (FY2025: brokers' EBITDA CHF 44.2m against company core CHF 48.8m; brokers' EPS CHF 0.184 against core CHF 0.49).
  4. No hedging by policy, so reported results move with the franc and with the revaluation of about CHF 200m of intercompany loans.
  5. Thin and partly invisible coverage. Five houses are named (ZKB, UBS, Stifel, Research Partners, Octavian; Berenberg, Vontobel and Baader also appear in one source); FactSet shows two. Non-deal roadshows after H1 2026 (ZKB in Geneva and London, UBS in Paris) suggest active effort to widen the shareholder base.
C-74

Judgement. Neither. Estimates were cut deeply for FY2025 and modestly for FY2026 after the March 2026 profit miss, but they are now rising: FY2027 EPS revisions are +15% over one month, +15% over three, +18% over six and +5% over twelve; EBITDA +9%, +10%, +12%, +22%; sales +4%, +8%, +7%, +9%; target prices +12%, +26%, +32%. The sell-side earnings momentum letter is B (pinned, dashboard). Guidance has been raised, not rebased; the only "sandbagging" is the conservative top-line range and the flat margin guide. One discordant signal: the buy-rating share fell from 100% to 75% over three months, so one house moved off Buy while others raised targets, likely on valuation. The stock is therefore in an upgrade cycle from a raised bar, not in a clearing set-up from a low bar.
