Capitalised development CHF 12.5m "capitalised" CHF 4.3m a year; 12.5m is the balance Set 2 is right

Two facts both sets treat as settled that are not.

  1. Who runs the US. Both sets build on "the finance director is US head ad interim" (Set 1's C-11 and C-86; Set 2's margin bridge even budgets for a new hire). The source is a single reading of one slide in the H1 2026 presentation, in a document that mis-dated it. The research commissioned to settle it found David Thoni listed as US President in March 2026 and no announcement of a departure or a successor through August 2026, and no reported distributor losses in 2026. I rate the interim arrangement as more likely than not (roughly 60%), but it is unverified in either direction. What is verified is the outcome so far: US organic growth of 27.4% in H1 2026.
  2. What a US TOUCH procedure is worth. Set 2's revenue model prices it at USD 6,500. The chief executive's planning figure five months earlier was USD 4,000 to 4,500, and the list price is USD 8,985. The model's most concentrated driver therefore has an unverified price.

One further incongruence, between the forecasts and the valuation frame. Set 1 described the shares as trading on "about 30 times" forward earnings, at the bottom of their own range; that multiple rests on the two brokers' EPS. On the model's own EPS the same share price is 48 times two-year-forward earnings (about 52 times on my changed inputs), which is between the median and the upper quartile of the stock's history (D-07). The memo should not use "cheap against its own history" and "our EPS is 35% below the brokers" in the same argument without reconciling them.

What this means for predictability. The congruence of the architecture raises confidence in the direction of every line. The factual corrections lower confidence in the level of EPS and show that the forecast's weakest inputs sit where disclosure is thinnest.

Why it is not placed It rates how well the sell-side and investors already articulate the case, and none of the eleven rates the narrative or how much of the case is known.

I-03

How comprehensively are these dynamics articulated in the current narrative of (i) sell-side commentary and (ii) investor commentary (for example on Twitter): how well known is each of them to investors who own or cover the company or its sector, primarily for the next 18 months and secondarily for 18 months to three years out?

F368th of 9

Layer 4 · Stock market narrative

Judgement. The narrative is comprehensive on the dynamics that are favourable and thin on the dynamics that are not. What is well known is good news: US TOUCH adoption ahead of plan, the raised and soon-to-be-beaten growth guidance, the end of tariff cost, a rebuilt US channel. It is known to everyone who follows the stock, it is in the brokers' numbers, and it is in a share price up 45% in a year. What is little known is mostly bad news for EPS over the next 6 to 18 months: a tax rate still at 46% and the reason for it, the tax rate the FY2026 number needs, how little the US grew outside TOUCH, the doubling of bank debt in six months, and the fact that "cheap against its own history" depends on estimates we think are 20% to 37% too high. The asymmetry is the finding: the surprises still available to this narrative are skewed towards the earnings lines and towards disappointment, with a small number of genuine positives that nobody is yet discussing.

The limits of the evidence, stated first. (i) Sell-side commentary: the research found no Medartis note from any of the covering houses. What the sell-side thinks is inferred from the two brokers' numbers and targets in FactSet, one rating change, and questions on the results calls. (ii) Investor commentary: none was found, on social media or elsewhere. For a company of this size, trading about CHF 1.5m a day and 39.9% owned by its founder, I take that at face value: there is very little public investor debate, and the narrative is in practice set by the company's own releases and two or three brokers. A thin narrative is easily moved, in both directions: in July 2026 the previous memo recorded sell-side momentum of D and coverage as "absent"; four months later it is B with targets raised by a third.

What is not in the narrative and would be good news.

  1. A change in the margin wording on 2 March 2027 (my 35% case). It would be the first sign of the one strength the market most needs to see, and no one can have priced a signal that has never been given.
  2. The length of the TOUCH payment window. Pass-through lasts to the end of 2028, a full three years. Anyone working with "two to three years", as the earlier sets did, has the price risk a year too early.
  3. No rival, and a higher bar for the next one. No US dual-mobility thumb prosthesis is in development that the research could find, and the pathway TOUCH used has closed to new applicants.
  4. Disclosure itself. If the company began to report TOUCH revenue and margin, the market could value it as what it is, a Bonesupport-like product (Bonesupport: P/E 33.5 times, the only other stock in the sector at Medartis's level). A capital markets day would be the natural occasion.
  5. The two technical corrections (conversion is EPS-neutral; enterprise value is overstated in databases).

These are real, but they are smaller and less certain than the negatives, and only the first falls inside the next six months.

The market's own readings (dashboard, 15 and 16 November 2026). Technical momentum D, although the price is 28% above its 200-day average and relative strength is in the 88th percentile, which marks the move as extended; sell-side earnings momentum B; valuation A on the brokers' EPS. Target prices of CHF 120 and CHF 129 are 10% and 18% above the price: even the two brokers, on numbers we think too high, see limited upside. One house has moved off Buy.

How the market has treated this mixture before. On the FY2025 results in March 2026 revenue beat, net profit missed by 36% and EPS forecasts were cut by double digits; the shares nonetheless stand 45% higher than a year ago. While growth is accelerating, this market has looked through misses below EBIT. That is the strongest argument against over-weighting the EPS gap in the next six months, and it should be in the memo. The same record implies where the vulnerability lies: at the point where growth stops accelerating and the earnings lines disappoint together, which on our forecasts is FY2027, with the first lower opening guide in March 2028.

Secondarily: 18 months to three years out. The long-run story the brokers tell (growth in the high teens, an EBITDA margin of 21% to 22%, EPS above CHF 5) is known and in their numbers. What is absent from any narrative we can see is everything dated 2029: the end of pass-through and the US TOUCH price reset, the put and call on the convertible, the NeoOrtho payment, and a tax rate that we think is still in the mid-20s. None of these is thesis-breaking; all of them are unpriced.

What each layer was asked, what its scores mean, and the Investment Analyst's notes and appendices

Layer 1: Assessing the foundational components of an investment case

Written 16 November 2026, 14:43 to 15:10 UK, Opus, main login

What the Investment Analyst was asked

The objective is to investigate and research each question, analyse the relevant information and form an insightful judgement regarding it, and to do the same for each group of questions as a whole, so as to best help the Consumer achieve their objective, which is to produce a well-reasoned, coherent, deeply understood and accurate operating model and financial model for the company for the next 18 months to four years.

What the scores mean in this layer

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

The Investment Analyst's title
Medartis Holding AG (MED-CH): Set 1 answers, the foundational components of the investment case

Written 16 November 2026 for the Investment Analyst who answers Set 2 (the operating and financial model, 18 months to four years). Scores run 1 to 100; higher is more positive for an investment case.

The Investment Analyst's opening notes

Preface: what the Consumer must know before using these answers

1. Source check (the instruction's caution). The "latest Earnings Review" (file 05) does cover the half-year to 30 June 2026, reported on 18 August 2026, the period FactSet shows as last reported. It also carries one post-period fact: a press release of 2 November 2026 reporting more than 2,000 US TOUCH surgeries by October and stating the group is "well positioned to exceed its 2026 financial forecast". The earlier Earnings Review (file 06) covers FY2025, reported 17 March 2026. The memo (file 22) is dated 19 July 2026 and pre-dates the H1 2026 results; several of its readings (sell-side momentum D, "coverage absent", price CHF 89.40) are now out of date.

2. Known errors and inconsistencies in the sources; do not model from them.

  • EMEA H1 2026 growth of "42.5% at constant currency" (file 05, section 6) is wrong; the correct figure is 17.7% organic (CHF 95.0m against CHF 67.9m reported, the gap being KeriMedical consolidation and currency).
  • File 05 says "as of January 2027" the finance director Peter Hackel is US head ad interim. The date is in the future and is an error, but the source cited is the H1 2026 results presentation of 18 August 2026, so the substance (the US President role is held ad interim by the finance director, and David Thoni, US President since January 2025, appears no longer in post) should be treated as probable but unconfirmed. This matters a great deal (see C-11 and C-86).
  • Selling and distribution costs falling from 46.4% of sales (FY2024) to 37.7% (FY2025) is too large a move to be operational; part of it is likely reclassification (CHF 3.6m to CHF 4.7m of distributor commissions were moved between net sales deductions and operating costs). Do not model a 870 basis point structural fall in selling costs.
  • FY2025 currency translation is given as both -4.4 percentage points and -8.0 percentage points of revenue growth; the core EBITDA margin currency drag as both -1.2 and -1.4 points. Use -4.4 points (reported group sales +21.2% against +25.6% at constant currency) and -1.2 points.
  • The Change Forces report's semi-annual table shows H1 2024 and H1 2025 with identical revenue (CHF 123.0m); it is corrupted. Use FactSet: H1 2024 CHF 112m, H2 2024 CHF 113m, H1 2025 CHF 123m, H2 2025 CHF 146m, H1 2026 CHF 161m.

3. The base numbers the Consumer should anchor on (company "core" basis unless stated).

Item FY2023 FY2024 FY2025 H1 2025 H1 2026

Annual convertible cost CHF 6.8m (CHF 3.5m cash coupon, CHF 3.3m non-cash accretion). Price CHF 109.00 (13 November 2026); market capitalisation CHF 1,604m on 14.7m diluted shares; enterprise value CHF 1,721m.

4. Consensus is two brokers. FactSet's consensus rests on UBS (Buy, target CHF 120) and Stifel (Buy, target CHF 129): FY2026 revenue CHF 342m, EBITDA CHF 64.8m, EPS CHF 1.55; FY2027 CHF 416m, CHF 83.7m, CHF 2.50; FY2028 CHF 489m, CHF 104m, CHF 3.79. Write "two brokers expect", not "consensus expects". The brokers' FY2025 base (EBITDA CHF 44.2m, EPS CHF 0.184) is on a different basis from the company's core figures (CHF 48.8m, CHF 0.49), so the "+742% EPS growth" is a basis artefact; compare consensus margins with the brokers'-basis FY2025 EBITDA margin of 16.4%, or rebuild from the company's core figures.

5. The single most important analytical fact for the model. In the two years under the present chief executive, organic growth has accelerated (11.7% to 15.7% to 17.0%) but operating leverage has not appeared: the incremental core EBITDA margin was about 15% in FY2025 (CHF 7.1m of extra EBITDA on CHF 46.5m of extra core sales) and about 19% in H1 2026 (CHF 7.1m on CHF 38.1m), below the group's own average margin. Currency, tariffs, the dilutive NeoOrtho value range, capacity built ahead of demand at five plants, and deliberate reinvestment in US training and sales absorbed it. The sell-side's FY2027 and FY2028 numbers require incremental EBITDA margins of about 25% to 28% and incremental EBIT margins above 20%. Whether that leverage finally shows is the fulcrum of the next four years, and these answers return to it repeatedly.

The Investment Analyst's closing notes

Overall steer to the Set 2 Analyst (synthesis, not a scored question)

  1. Revenue: model by region and product. US and KeriMedical/TOUCH as separate high-growth lines (US organic in the 20s in 2026/27; TOUCH units in the thousands, ASP to be researched); EMEA plating at low-to-mid teens (lower in H1 2027 against the weather comparison); APAC and LATAM at high single digits with policy volatility. Group organic about 19% to 20% in H2 2026, mid-to-high teens in 2027, low-to-mid teens by 2028.
  2. Gross margin: recovery towards 82% to 83% by 2027/28 (tariffs, Warsaw, TOUCH mix, Titan Nail), minus NeoOrtho and the franc.
  3. Operating costs: build fixed and variable separately; run reinvest-all, partial-release and full-release cases; the brokers sit near partial release.
  4. Below EBIT: convertible cost CHF 6.8m a year (or conversion: about one million extra shares); FX on intercompany loans as an explicit volatile line; a normalised tax rate as an assumption, flagged.
  5. Cash: NWC at 30% to 35% of incremental sales, capex about 10% of sales in 2026 easing to 6% to 8%; FCF near zero in 2026 and modest in 2027.
  6. Tests to carry: the false-friend hypothesis on cash (not margins); the US leadership gap; the FY2027 guide on 2 March 2027; the TOUCH US average selling price; and the precedent that a halving of growth has cost Swiss orthopaedic peers 25% to 40% of their share price.
Appendix Research requests the Investment Analyst raised for Layer 2

Research requests for the next set

Company: Medartis Holding AG (MED-CH), the Swiss maker of titanium implants, instruments and joint prostheses for hand, wrist, elbow, shoulder, foot and ankle and cranio-maxillofacial surgery, run as three brands (Medartis premium fixation; KeriMedical, maker of the TOUCH thumb carpometacarpal prosthesis, 100% owned since July 2025; NeoOrtho, a 51%-owned Brazilian value-implant maker) plus CADskills patient-specific 3D-printed implants; it reports Upper Extremities, Lower Extremities and CMF & Others segments and four regions (EMEA, US, APAC, LATAM), semi-annually, on a "core" basis, with a Swiss-franc cost base, production in Basel, Warsaw (Indiana), Archamps and Besançon (France), Curitiba (Brazil) and Gent (Belgium), and no currency hedging.

Request: T-P1

Peers: Medacta (MOVE-CH; Swiss orthopaedic specialist with the same franc cost base, consignment instrument-set model and education-led surgeon conversion, the closest margin and capital-intensity precedent); Straumann (STMN-CH; Swiss premium implant maker that scaled operating margin with share gains and runs the Neodent value brand the Medartis chief executive previously led); Stryker (leader in trauma and extremities after Wright Medical, launching the Pangea plating system in Europe); Zimmer Biomet including Paragon 28 (a foot-and-ankle specialist absorbed into a major, a precedent for specialist margins at scale); Globus Medical (fast-growing trauma entrant with high margins and 70% insourced production); Integra LifeSciences (extremities exposure, cost and plant remediation precedent); Treace Medical and Enovis (US extremities specialists with high selling-cost ratios); Smith & Nephew (cost programme precedent under a scale disadvantage); Acumed and Arthrex (private US extremities rivals; cost structure as far as known). Inputs: What is the split of Medartis's cost base between fixed and variable costs: cost of goods, distributor commissions and other variable selling costs, field sales and education, R&D (expensed and capitalised; capitalised development was CHF 12.5m in 2025 against CHF 9.0m in 2024, annual report), general and administration, and manufacturing overhead by plant? What incremental core EBITDA and core EBIT margin did the company earn on organic revenue growth in 2019, 2021, 2023, 2024, 2025 and H1 2026, and what did management say absorbed the operating leverage in each year (currency, US tariffs, capacity built ahead of demand, NeoOrtho value-segment dilution, US training and commercial investment)? How did margins behave in the 2019 growth shortfall (EBITDA margin from about 15% to 13%), the 2020 Covid volume fall, the 2023 ransomware production halt and the 2025 tariff and franc shock? How much of the fall in selling and distribution costs from 46.4% of sales in FY2024 to 37.7% in FY2025 (company) reflects reclassification of distributor commissions rather than operating improvement? What is the utilisation of the Basel, Warsaw, Archamps/Besançon and Curitiba plants after the 2025/26 capacity programme, what fixed overhead was added, and how did management describe the gross-margin effect of capacity built ahead of demand in 2025? What will Project Flash (70% of US volume made in Warsaw by end-2026 and about 80% in 2027, company, August 2026) save against tariff costs of CHF 3.0m in 2025 and about CHF 4.0m guided for 2026, and when do those savings reach the income statement given the 6 to 10 month inventory lag? What synergies will the Cold Fusion consolidation of Medartis Brazil and NeoOrtho in Curitiba deliver from 2027, and on what timetable? What capex and instrument-set investment is needed after 2026, and how fast does depreciation of sets and plants rise? Which cost lines did Medacta, Straumann, Globus and Smith & Nephew find cuttable or scalable, and at what revenue level did specialist orthopaedic companies' selling-cost ratios begin to fall? What have management and brokers (UBS, Stifel, ZKB, Octavian, Research Partners) said about how much of the extra gross profit from 17% to 19% organic growth (2026 guidance, 18 August 2026) will be reinvested rather than reach the core EBITDA margin guided at "high teens"? Focus: Whether Medartis's core EBITDA margin (17.1% in 2023, 19.0% in 2024, 18.4% in 2025, 18.0% in H1 2026, company) will expand materially in 2027 to 2029 as tariffs fall away, the plant programme ends and US selling costs scale, or whether management will keep reinvesting the operating leverage; and which cost lines would have to be held flat for the margin to reach the 20% to 21% that UBS and Stifel forecast for FY2027 and FY2028 (FactSet, November 2026).

Request: T-P2

Peers: Acumed (private; incumbent in volar wrist plates, launched Acu-Loc NEXT in December 2025 and the ASCent fixed-price programme for surgery centres); Stryker (trauma and extremities leader, bundled hospital contracts, Pangea plating); Johnson & Johnson's DePuy Synthes (trauma leader exposed to volume-based procurement, separating by mid-2027); Zimmer Biomet and Paragon 28 (foot and ankle pricing under a major); Globus Medical (trauma share gainer); Skeletal Dynamics (private US upper-extremity specialist); KLS Martin and Materialise (CMF and patient-specific implant pricing); Medacta (premium Swiss specialist pricing against majors); Straumann (premium and value-brand dual pricing, Neodent); Bonesupport (a reimbursed specialist consumable whose US uptake was slowed by hospital cost containment in 2026); value-tier makers Hofer, GMReis and Orthotech (Latin American and emerging-market tender pricing). Operating conditions: US group purchasing organisation and integrated delivery network contracts with multi-year terms and automatic price-downs; hospital value analysis committee approvals and administrator cost containment; migration of hand and foot procedures to ambulatory surgery centres and fixed-price procedure bundles; government-mandated price cuts (Australia's cuts of 12%, 5% and 5% between 2022 and July 2025; Thailand); European public tenders and reimbursement reform (Germany's hybrid DRG system, declining Swiss hospital reimbursement); Latin American public tenders; US import tariffs needing pass-through (10% to 39% to about 15% in 2025); a strong Swiss franc; the launch of a newly reimbursed device (TOUCH: FDA approval July 2025, a new US CPT code, Medicare transitional pass-through payment from 1 January 2026, Australian Prescribed List reimbursement at about CHF 1,300 from 1 July 2026); competitor launches reaching product parity in wrist and elbow plating. Inputs: What average selling price does TOUCH achieve in the US and in Europe, how is it paid under Medicare pass-through status, when does that status expire and what payment applies afterwards? How much of the selective US price increase of about 3% in early 2026 (company) was realised, given that about 60% of US sales are under multi-year GPO contracts and price reviews were expected to fund about one third of the tariff burden? How large are the annual automatic price-downs in US GPO and European hospital contracts for hand, wrist and foot plating, and has Medartis's realised price in plating risen, held or fallen since 2022 by region? Is the 10% to 15% price premium over generalist trauma suppliers that Medartis claims visible in tender and contract data? What happened to Medartis's Australian revenue, volumes and margins through the 2022 to 2025 cuts, and what is the Australian TOUCH price and volume since July 2026? How do NeoOrtho's tender prices compare with Medartis brand prices in Brazil, and is there cannibalisation? How did Medartis's prices behave after the move to direct sales in Japan (2024) and Spain (2021)? Do implant prices in hand and trauma fall when procedure volumes fall (2009, 2020)? How does Acumed's ASCent fixed-price bundle compare with per-implant pricing, and are surgery centres switching? What do surgeons, hospital buyers, distributors and brokers say now about premium extremity implant pricing and about a competing US thumb-base prosthesis? Focus: How much of Medartis's revenue growth and gross-margin improvement over the next four years can come from price and mix rather than volume, in particular the durability of TOUCH pricing once US pass-through status ends, and whether plating prices hold against GPO price-downs, surgery-centre bundles and competitor parity launches.

Request: T-P3 (only where your answers show this report would be useful for this company)

Why requested: Medartis's next four years depend on whether its reinforcing growth loops (surgeon training, exclusive distribution, TOUCH cross-selling) outrun the balancing loops that have so far absorbed its growth (working capital and set capital, plant overhead, buyer power and the franc), which decides when margins and cash flow inflect. Peers: Straumann (STMN-CH; education-led share-gain loop via the ITI network and a value-brand loop via Neodent); Medacta (MOVE-CH; surgeon education loop through the M.O.R.E. Institute and a capital-intensive set loop with low free cash flow conversion); Paragon 28 under Zimmer Biomet (a specialist dedicated-channel loop absorbed by a major); Acumed (private; incumbent loop in wrist plating and its response through acquisitions and fixed-price bundles); Bonesupport (a reimbursement-created demand loop slowed by hospital administrators); Globus Medical (a fast trauma share-gain loop). Inputs: Does Medartis's surgeon education (1,450 professionals trained through IBRA in 2025; 330 US hand surgeons targeted in 2026; the Orlando office and the M.A.R.C. Institute in Miami, acquired September 2026) convert into new accounts and implant pull-through, with what lag, and what does Straumann's ITI or Medacta's M.O.R.E. record show about that lag? Do TOUCH thumb prosthesis accounts (more than 2,000 US surgeries by October 2026, company, 2 November 2026) adopt the APTUS plating range, and is there evidence of cross-selling in account data? Do exclusive US distributors (sales up 46% against 16% for multiline partners, company) keep compounding share, and what makes exclusive agents leave? Does each new account require instrument sets and inventory that tie up working capital (inventory about 36% of sales; working capital up CHF 25.0m in H1 2026, company) and so limit the cash available to fund further growth? Does capacity built ahead of demand at five or six plants raise fixed costs that only scale can absorb, creating a loop in which slower growth lowers margins further? Do hospital group purchasing and value analysis committees slow adoption as Medartis's US share rises? Does franc strength, by depressing reported earnings, feed back into investment choices, financing costs or the valuation? Does local production in Warsaw reduce both tariff and currency exposure in a self-reinforcing way? Focus: Which of these loops is operating now on dated evidence, and whether the balancing loops (working capital and set capital, plant overhead, buyer power, currency) are strong enough to stop the training and TOUCH share-gain loop from reaching operating margin and free cash flow by 2027 or 2028.

Request: T-P4 (only where your answers show this report would be useful for this company)

Why requested: Medartis's earnings per share are small relative to its revenue and are dominated by items below operating profit (convertible costs, currency on intercompany loans, an erratic tax rate, capitalised development and minorities), so the model needs each input established from evidence rather than assumed. Peers: Medacta (MOVE-CH; Swiss franc cost base with no formal hedges, a direct comparison for currency sensitivity and below-EBIT items); Straumann (STMN-CH; Swiss franc reporter with transaction hedging, a contrast in hedging policy and tax rate); Sonova (Swiss franc reporter, hedging and tax precedent); Ypsomed (Swiss medtech with a domestic cost base); Integra LifeSciences (uses Swiss franc to US dollar cross-currency swaps); Stryker and Globus Medical (hedging programmes for comparison). Inputs: What are the conversion price, conversion conditions, call provisions and accounting treatment of the CHF 115.8m 3.0% convertible bond due 2031 (issued April 2024), how many shares would conversion create, and how would it change interest cost (CHF 3.5m cash coupon and CHF 3.3m non-cash accretion in 2025, company)? What currency composition do Medartis's revenue and costs have (franc, US dollar, euro, Australian dollar, Japanese yen, Brazilian real), what are the translation and transaction sensitivities (annual report: a 10% USD/CHF move about CHF 1.9m of pre-tax profit; a 10% AUD/CHF move about CHF 1.3m), how large are intercompany loans (more than CHF 200m) and how did their revaluation produce CHF 5.2m of FX losses in 2025 and gains in H1 2026? What do published bank forecasts for USD/CHF, EUR/CHF and AUD/CHF for 2027 and 2028 imply? What is the normal effective tax rate on core profits, why did it swing (transfer-pricing adjustments in 2025), and what do management and brokers assume? How much development cost is capitalised each year and how fast is it amortised? What share of NeoOrtho profit goes to minorities until the remaining 49% is bought for about CHF 15.4m in 2028/29? What net interest, lease and bank-facility costs apply, and how much of the undrawn credit lines (more than CHF 50m at end-2025) is available? What share count, employee share issuance and conditional capital apply? How do UBS and Stifel (FY2027 EPS forecasts of CHF 2.50 each, FactSet, 3 November 2026) bridge from core EBIT to EPS, what currency and tax assumptions do they use, and how did FY2025 consensus EPS fall from above CHF 1 to about CHF 0.18 during 2025? Focus: What FY2027 and FY2028 earnings per share look like once the convertible, currency on intercompany loans, tax, capitalised development and minorities are modelled explicitly, and which of these inputs most widens the range of outcomes.

Layer 2: Translating the foundational components into an operating and financial model for the next 18 months, and 18 months to 3 years out

Written 17 November 2026, 17:49 to 18:09 UK, Opus, main login

What the Investment Analyst was asked

The objective is to produce a well-reasoned, coherent, deeply understood and accurate operating and financial model for the company for the next 18 months to four years, based on the first set's answers, a base of the company's historical financial and operating performance, and how businesses in the sector and industry outperform or underperform, so as to best help the Consumer achieve their objective, which is to produce a trustworthy judgement regarding the predictability of delivery of the financial forecasts over the next 18 months.

What the scores mean in this layer

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the likely effect on the company's financial forecasts is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

The Investment Analyst's title
Medartis Holding AG (MED-CH): Set 2 answers, the operating and financial model (18 months to four years)

Written 17 November 2026 for the Investment Analyst who answers Sets 3 and 4. Scores run 1 to 100; for this set a score says how positive the likely effect on the company's financial forecasts is for an investment case (50 = neither). Money in CHF millions unless stated; per-share figures in CHF.

The Investment Analyst's opening notes

Preface: what the Consumer must know before using these answers

  1. The basis of every figure here

  • All profit figures are the company's "core" basis (it excludes acquisition revaluation gains, inventory step-up, amortisation of acquired intangibles, transaction and legal costs and pension past-service items), expressed as a percentage of IFRS net sales, because IFRS net sales is the revenue line in the financial model and in FactSet. On this basis the FY2025 core EBITDA margin is 18.1% (the company quotes 18.4% on core sales of CHF 266.1m) and H1 2026 is 17.9% (company 18.0%).
  • Earnings per share use about 12.4m to 12.6m basic shares. The company's own basic EPS implies a denominator of about 12.4m (FY2025 reported EPS CHF 0.673 on net income CHF 8.3m; H1 2026 CHF 0.412 on CHF 5.1m), and consensus net income divided by consensus EPS gives the same 12.4m in each year. The 13.63m shares registered at end-2025 and FactSet's 14.7m "diluted" count are not the EPS denominator. The roughly 1.2m gap is most probably treasury shares; the Consumer should confirm it in the FY2025 annual report's EPS note (a research request asks for it).
  • Free cash flow (my definition) is core EBITDA less non-core cash costs, working capital, cash tax, cash interest, capital spending including intangibles, and lease payments; it is before acquisitions. It is stricter than the company's definition (which is before lease payments) and FactSet's (which is also before intangible capex). Part 4 of the forecast inputs gives the bridge.

  1. Where I differ from the Set 1 answers, and why

  1. Bank debt is about CHF 32m at June 2026, not CHF 12m. FactSet's capital-structure snapshot shows debt of CHF 130m, which is the convertible's CHF 98.1m carrying value plus about CHF 32m; the H1 2026 cash bridge (cash down only CHF 7.2m despite free cash flow of CHF -10.1m, lease payments of about CHF 4.5m and CHF 14.9m of acquisition payments) also needs about CHF 20m of new borrowing. Net financial debt excluding leases and the NeoOrtho put is therefore about CHF 104m at June 2026 (Set 1: CHF 84m), about 1.9 times trailing core EBITDA. Confidence medium; a request asks for the debt note.
  2. The convertible is EPS-accretive if converted, not dilutive. Its terms are now known (AlphaSense EPS-bridge report, from the annual reports): conversion price CHF 100.725 (in the money at CHF 109), 1,149,665 shares, issuer call at par from 2 May 2029 if the share price is at or above CHF 130.94 on 20 of 30 trading days, maturity 11 April 2031. Conversion removes about CHF 7m a year of cost (about CHF 5.4m after tax, about CHF 0.43 a share) while adding about 9% more shares; at any EPS below about CHF 4.7 that raises EPS. Under IAS 33 the bond is antidilutive and excluded from diluted EPS until basic EPS exceeds about CHF 4.7. Set 1's "7% to 8% EPS dilution" is wrong in EPS terms; the dilution is to ownership, not to earnings.
  3. Capitalised development is CHF 4.3m a year, not CHF 12.5m. CHF 12.5m is the net book value at end-2025; additions were CHF 4.3m in 2025 against CHF 2.0m in 2024, with only CHF 0.8m amortised. The practice flatters EBITDA by about CHF 3.5m a year (about 1.3 points of margin) against expensing, and amortisation will rise; it is a quality issue of modest, not large, size.
  4. The tariff tailwind into 2027 is smaller than Set 1's 0.8 points. The H1 2026 core tariff charge was only CHF 0.9m after a CHF 4.4m refund receivable (CHF 1.7m of it reversed 2025 expense) (AlphaSense operating-leverage report). With 70% of US volume made locally by end-2026 and about 80% by mid-2027, the 2027 tariff cost should be about CHF 0.5m to 1.5m against roughly CHF 2m to 3m in 2026: a tailwind of about 0.3 to 0.5 points of margin, not 0.8. Confidence medium.
  5. The core tax rate has been about 46%, not a normal 22% to 26%. FY2025 core tax was CHF 5.2m on core pre-tax profit of about CHF 11.3m (core EBIT CHF 24.5m, core net profit CHF 6.1m), and the same derivation gives about 45% to 50% for H1 2026. Management guides 18% to 19% long term and calls sell-side rates above 19% too conservative; there are no advance pricing agreements. This is the largest single EPS swing factor in 2026 and 2027.
  6. TOUCH's US projection for 2026 is now about 3,000 procedures (company, 2 November 2026, as reported in the AlphaSense pricing report), up from 1,800 at H1 and 1,200 at the start; management cited a stable US average selling price of about USD 6,500 (August 2026; a single source, medium confidence).
  7. The NeoOrtho put is CHF 18.4m at June 2026 (CHF 16.4m at end-2025), undiscounted up to CHF 24.8m, payable in FY2029 on FY2028 sales; fair-value changes go through equity, not the income statement.
  8. Consensus EBITDA should be read against core, not against FactSet's "brokers' basis" actual. FactSet records FY2025 brokers'-basis EBITDA of CHF 44.2m (16.4%), which is not the company's core CHF 48.8m; the brokers' FY2026 forecast of CHF 64.8m (19.0%) is consistent with "high teens core", so the forecasts look core-like. Against core, consensus implies FY2026 EBITDA growth of about 33%, not 47%.
  9. Operating leverage: Set 1 framed the brokers' 25% to 28% incremental margins as undemonstrated. I find them close to achievable (P-01): over FY2019 to FY2025 the incremental core EBITDA margin averaged about 21.5%, and 2027/28 have identifiable mechanical tailwinds. My gap to consensus comes from revenue, depreciation and tax, not from a lower leverage rate.
  10. FactSet's enterprise value of CHF 1,721m counts the convertible twice (as 1.15m extra shares inside the 14.7m and as debt inside the CHF 130m) and includes the treasury shares. On 12.45m basic shares with the convertible as debt, enterprise value is about CHF 1,475m; treating it as equity because it is in the money, about CHF 1,500m. Valuation multiples are about 13% lower than FactSet shows (relevant to Set 4).

  1. The base case at a glance

Part 4. The reasoning behind the inputs

Segments: revenue and margin by segment, where the company reports them

Medartis reports three product groups (Upper Extremities; Lower Extremities; CMF and Others) and four regions (EMEA, North America, APAC, LATAM), semi-annually. It discloses gross profit by region but no segment EBITDA or EBIT.

Margins by segment: FY2025 regional gross margin (company segment note) was EMEA 78.4%, North America 83.3%, APAC 87.5%, LATAM 70.0% (NeoOrtho value implants dilute LATAM), before CHF 9.5m of unallocated cost. Upper Extremities grew 21.1% organically in H1 2026 and Lower Extremities 10.4%; mix moves towards Upper Extremities (71% of core sales in H1 2026, about 77% by FY2030 in my base) and North America (19% of sales in FY2025, about 27% by FY2028), both above the group's gross margin, which supports the gross-margin rise from 81.8% (FY2026) to 83.0% (FY2030). The two brokers' Upper Extremities path (+27%, +22%) implies a larger TOUCH ramp than mine; my Lower Extremities growth is lower than theirs because of competitive pressure (Zimmer Biomet with Paragon 28) and the 2027 NX-to-Titan Nail transition.

The operating drivers behind the inputs

  • Revenue. Organic growth (core, constant currency) of about 19.5% to 20% in FY2026 (H1 17.0%; H2 about 21% to 22% as KeriMedical enters the organic base, the US laps the 2025 Florida distributor termination, and US TOUCH reaches about 2,100 procedures in the half), about 17% in FY2027, about 14.5% in FY2028, 13% in FY2029 and 12% in FY2030. Acquisitions add about 9 points in FY2026 (KeriMedical in H1, NeoOrtho, CADskills from May 2026, M.A.R.C. Institute from September 2026) and 0.5 point in FY2027. Currency costs about 2.5 points in FY2026 and 1 point in FY2027 (franc firm against the euro and Australian dollar); constant currency thereafter.
  • US TOUCH, the most concentrated driver: about 3,000 US procedures in 2026 (company projection, November 2026), 7,000 in 2027, 10,000 in 2028, 12,500 in 2029, 15,000 in 2030, at a US selling price of about USD 6,500 (management, August 2026) held to 2027 and reduced 5% in each of 2028 and 2029 as Medicare transitional pass-through (from 1 January 2026, typically two to three years) ends. US TOUCH revenue about CHF 15m to 16m (2026), CHF 37m (2027), CHF 51m (2028). It drives North America growth of about 33% (2026), 37% (2027) and 28% (2028), supported by 330 US hand surgeons trained in 2026, the M.A.R.C. Institute and the Orlando centre, and exclusive distributors (46% growth against 16% for multiline partners).
  • EMEA grows about 13% organic in FY2027 (tough H1 comparison after winter-trauma-boosted H1 2026; KeriMedical's European TOUCH now organic) and 10% to 11% after; APAC 7% to 9% (Australian TOUCH listing from July 2026; Japan approvals pending); LATAM about 10% to 11% (NeoOrtho value tenders, Mexico and other entries), less currency.
  • Price is about -0.5% to 0% a year like-for-like in plating (GPO automatic price-downs of 1% to 3% on about 62% of US sales; European and LATAM tenders); mix adds 1 to 2 points a year to 2028.
  • Gross margin (core) 81.8%, 82.3%, 82.6%, 82.8%, 83.0%: tariff cost falling as Project Flash reaches 70% of US volume made locally by end-2026 and about 80% by mid-2027 (2027 tailwind about 0.3 to 0.5 points, because refunds already cut the 2026 charge), TOUCH mix, Warsaw absorption, Titan Nail replacing the distributed NX nail, partly offset by NeoOrtho and plating price-downs.
  • EBITDA margin (core EBITDA on IFRS net sales; FY2025 18.1%, H1 2026 17.9%): 18.6% in FY2026, then about 90 basis points a year to 22.2% in FY2030. Incremental EBITDA margins of about 25% (FY2027) and 28% to 29% (FY2028-30), against a FY2019-FY2025 average of about 21.5%; operating cost excluding D&A falls from 63.2% to 60.8% of sales, mainly through the selling and distribution ratio (37.1% in H1 2026) falling towards 34%. Half of identifiable efficiencies (Cold Fusion in LATAM from 2027, Titan Nail, Warsaw) is assumed reinvested. Within the next 18 months: H2 2026 19.3% (tariff cost lower than in H1; seasonally stronger half; currency drag 90 to 100 basis points), H1 2027 18.8%, H2 2027 20.1%.
  • D&A (excluding acquired-intangible amortisation of about CHF 2m a year): H1 2026 about CHF 14.4m; CHF 29.5m in FY2026 rising to CHF 45.5m in FY2030 (8.7% falling to 8.0% of sales) after the 2025-2027 plant and instrument-set programme, including right-of-use depreciation on leases of about CHF 10m to 12m a year and rising amortisation of capitalised development (CHF 4.3m capitalised in 2025).
  • Net financial items: convertible CHF 115.8m at 3.0% (cash coupon CHF 3.5m plus non-cash accretion CHF 3.3m to 3.7m a year), bank borrowing of about CHF 32m at June 2026 rising to about CHF 45m to 50m in 2027 (about 2% to 2.5%), lease interest about CHF 1.0m to 1.4m, other about CHF 0.3m. Currency on more than CHF 200m of intercompany loans: about CHF +3m for FY2026 (the H1 2026 gain of CHF 7.0m, partly offset; no further gain assumed in H2) and CHF -1m a year after (small negative bias; realistic band ±CHF 6m a year). The convertible is assumed to stay as debt to 2031 (see the guidance section on the call).
  • Tax rate on core pre-tax profit: 38% (FY2026; H1 2026 was about 45% to 50% by derivation, FY2025 about 46%), 28%, 25%, 23%, 22%: normalising as profits outgrow fixed tax leakage (unrecognised losses, transfer pricing), but more slowly than management's 18% to 19% long-term guidance.
  • Dividend: none (none ever paid; cash constrained). Share count: basic about 12.4m (the company's EPS denominator), plus about 0.03m to 0.04m a year from employee plans (36,737 shares issued in 2025; 13,836 in H1 2026). No buybacks or cash issuance assumed.
  • Capital spending (including capitalised development and instrument sets): about CHF 36m in FY2026 (CHF 17.9m in H1; tracking above the original CHF 25m guide; brokers CHF 35m) and FY2027 (new Besançon facility planned for H1 2028; set placements for the US), CHF 35m in FY2028, then about 7.3% to 7.5% of sales.
  • Working capital (receivables plus inventories less payables, FactSet definition): 49.2% of sales at end-2025 and 47.6% at June 2026; 44.5% at end-2026 (seasonal H2 release, collection discipline under the new finance director), then 44%, 43%, 42%, 41%: about a third of each franc of incremental sales.
  • Lease payments (principal and interest): CHF 9.1m in 2025, rising with new building leases (CHF 41.2m of building lease commitments at mid-2026) to CHF 13m by 2030.
  • Acquisitions: FY2026 CHF 19m (CHF 14.9m paid in H1 2026, largely CADskills and KeriMedical earn-out, plus the M.A.R.C. Institute); about CHF 2m to 3m a year of earn-outs and small deals; FY2029 CHF 20m for the remaining 49% of NeoOrtho (liability CHF 18.4m at June 2026, undiscounted up to CHF 24.8m, based on FY2028 sales).
  • Minorities (not an input line, but in my EPS): 49% of NeoOrtho's profit, about CHF 0.8m to 1.1m a year in 2026-2028, CHF 0.6m in 2029, nil in 2030.

The company's guidance, and how your inputs compare with it

  • FY2026 guidance: organic core sales growth of 17% to 19% (raised on 18 August 2026 from 16% to 18%) and a core EBITDA margin "in the high teens at constant exchange rates", with a spot-rate currency drag of 90 to 100 basis points; on 2 November 2026 the company said it was "well positioned to exceed its 2026 financial forecast" and raised its US TOUCH projection to about 3,000 procedures. My inputs: organic about 19.5% to 20% (above the top of the range, consistent with the November statement), reported +25.7%; core EBITDA margin 18.6% of IFRS sales (about 18.8% on core sales, about 19.5% at constant currency): inside "high teens", at its upper end.
  • FY2027: no guidance yet; the first FY2027 guide comes with the FY2026 results on 2 March 2027. The company's first guides have been conservative (13% to 15% for FY2025; 16% to 18% for FY2026) and raised at the half-year. I expect a first FY2027 guide of about 15% to 17% organic and a margin described as "high teens" or "around 19% to 20%" at constant currency. My inputs (about 17% organic; 19.5% margin) sit at or slightly above that likely guide, consistent with the raise-at-the-half pattern. The two brokers (FY2027 revenue CHF 416m, +21.6%; EBITDA margin 20.1%; EPS CHF 2.50) sit above both.
  • Mid-term: there are no formal mid-term financial targets; the stated ambition is US organic growth above 20% a year. My North America growth (33%, 37%, 28%, then about 21% and 16%) meets it to 2029.
  • Items without guidance: EPS, free cash flow, tax (management says 18% to 19% underlying long term; my path is above it), financial result, capital spending beyond 2026 and working capital.
  • The convertible call: the company may call the bond at par from 2 May 2029 if the share price is at or above CHF 130.94 on 20 of 30 trading days, which forces conversion into 1,149,665 shares (conversion price CHF 100.725). I put this at roughly even odds and have not put it into the inputs (the model has no line to remove the debt at the same time). If it happens in 2029: share count +1.15m, net financial items about CHF 7m a year less negative, debt about CHF 115m lower; EPS is neutral to about 1% higher.

The bridge from the company's own reported figures to the basis consensus uses

  1. Revenue. My revenue is IFRS net sales (FactSet's revenue line; FY2025 CHF 269.3m), which the brokers also use (FactSet's FY2025 brokers'-basis actual is CHF 269m). The company's "core sales" (CHF 266.1m in FY2025) exclude about CHF 3m of non-core sales (mostly the exited Warsaw contract manufacturing). Its "organic growth" is core sales at constant currency, with acquisitions excluded for their first twelve months (KeriMedical, consolidated in July 2025, entered the organic base in July 2026).
  2. EBITDA. My EBITDA margin is the company's core EBITDA divided by IFRS net sales. Core EBITDA excludes non-core items: in FY2025, IFRS EBITDA was CHF 51.5m against core CHF 48.8m (a CHF 14.5m gain on revaluing the earlier KeriMedical stake and a CHF 8.6m release of NSI contingent consideration excluded upwards; CHF 8.8m of inventory step-up, a CHF 6.6m pension past-service charge and transaction costs excluded downwards). FactSet's "operating" EBITDA (CHF 25.6m in FY2025) is a different standardised line and should not be compared. FactSet's brokers'-basis FY2025 actual (CHF 44.2m, 16.4%) does not match core; the brokers' FY2026 forecast (CHF 64.8m, 19.0%) is consistent with a core basis, so compare consensus with core: consensus FY2026 EBITDA growth is about +33% against core, not +47%.
  3. D&A. Mine excludes amortisation of acquired intangibles (about CHF 2m a year; CHF 1.0m in H1 2026), as core does. FactSet's consensus D&A (CHF 27.6m, 30.8m, 33.9m) looks low: twice the H1 2026 core run-rate (about CHF 14.4m) already exceeds the FY2026 figure, and the 2025-27 capex wave raises it further.
  4. Net financial items on the core basis include the currency result on intercompany loans and the convertible's non-cash accretion (neither is excluded from core).
  5. EPS. Mine is core net profit attributable to shareholders (after NeoOrtho minorities) divided by average basic shares of about 12.4m to 12.6m. The company's own basic EPS uses about 12.4m shares (FY2025 CHF 0.673 on IFRS net income CHF 8.3m; H1 2026 CHF 0.412 on CHF 5.1m), and consensus net income divided by consensus EPS gives the same 12.4m in every year, so the brokers use the same denominator; 13.63m registered shares (end-2025) presumably include about 1.2m treasury shares, and FactSet's 14.7m adds the 1.15m convertible shares. The convertible is antidilutive under IAS 33 (excluded from diluted EPS) until basic EPS exceeds about CHF 4.7. FactSet's FY2025 brokers'-basis EPS actual (CHF 0.184) is not core (core about CHF 0.49), so the "+742%" FY2026 consensus growth is a basis artefact; against core, consensus FY2026 EPS implies about +216%.
  6. IFRS against core. Non-core items (acquisition amortisation about CHF 2m a year, transaction and legal costs, step-ups) put IFRS EPS about CHF 0.1 to 0.2 below core EPS each year.
  7. Free cash flow. My figure is core EBITDA less non-core cash costs, working capital change, cash tax, cash interest (excluding accretion and lease interest), capital spending including intangibles and lease payments; before acquisitions. Add back lease principal (about CHF 9m to 12m a year) to reach the company's definition (operating cash flow less capex including intangibles: CHF 21.0m in FY2024, CHF 9.3m in FY2025, CHF -10.1m in H1 2026); add back intangible capex as well (about CHF 5m a year) to reach FactSet's definition, which is the basis of the consensus FCF (CHF 3.4m, 10.3m, 30.4m). On FactSet's definition my FCF is about CHF -4m (FY2026), CHF 12m (FY2027) and CHF 25m (FY2028), close to consensus.
  8. Enterprise value. FactSet's CHF 1,721m counts the convertible twice (in the 14.7m share count and in debt) and includes treasury shares; on 12.45m shares with the convertible as debt it is about CHF 1,475m.

Layer 3 and Layer 4: Assessing the predictability of the financial forecasts for the next 18 months / Assessing the extent to which our assessment of the foundational components and financial forecasts is reflected or not in current company guidance, sell-side estimates and stock market narrative, primarily for the next 18 months and secondarily for 18 months to 3 years out

Written 18 November 2026, 11:19 to 11:45 UK, Fable, main login

What the Investment Analyst was asked

The objective is to produce a robustly tested assessment of the predictability of the financial forecasts for the next 18 months, based on the first and second sets' answers.

The objective is to produce that judgement, based on the first and second sets' answers and your answers to the third set, for the Investment Analyst who writes the investment memo, so that Richard, who reads the memo, can form his judgement on the investment case.

What the scores mean in this layer

Finish each answer and each judgement on a group or set with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For the third set's questions, the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for the predictability of the company's financial forecasts over the next 18 months, and so for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

For the fourth set's questions, the score says how favourable for an investment case in the company the gap is between our assessment and what current guidance, sell-side estimates and the stock market narrative already reflect: 100 the most favourable, 1 the least, 50 neither.

Medartis Holding AG (MED-CH): Set 3 and Set 4 answers, the predictability of the forecasts and what guidance, the sell-side and the market already reflect

Written 18 November 2026 for the Investment Analyst who writes the investment memo. Scores run 1 to 100. Money in CHF millions unless stated; per-share figures in CHF. The Investment Analyst's opening notes

Preface: what the Consumer must know before using these answers

  1. Cautions on the AlphaSense reports written for this work

  • A "12% tax rate for 2026" appears in two reports (the tax report and the narrative report). Treat it as unverified and probably a misreading. The same reports give an H1 2026 core tax charge of CHF 5.7m on core pre-tax profit of CHF 12.4m, a rate of 46%, and 12.5% is the tariff rate the company expects on its US imports. Nothing in the first set's sources mentions a 12% tax rate. It matters a great deal, because a 12% to 13% tax rate in H2 2026 is exactly what the two brokers' FY2026 EPS needs (see I-02), so I ask for it to be checked (research request 1).
  • No report contains a research note on Medartis from any covering broker, and none contains any investor commentary (social media or otherwise). What the sell-side "knows" is therefore inferred from the brokers' numbers in FactSet and from company documents. I say so wherever it matters in I-03.
  • Several "findings" repeat figures that were in our own questions (the US TOUCH selling price of USD 6,500; the CHF 7.0m currency gain; the finance director as US head ad interim). They are not independent confirmation.
  • Small errors to ignore: Project Flash moves production to Warsaw, Indiana, not Poland; the local-production target for end-2026 is 70%, not 60%; EMEA's H1 2026 growth of "42.5% at constant currency" includes acquisitions (organic was 17.7%).

  1. New facts that change the picture (each used in the answers)

  1. TOUCH's US Medicare pass-through payment runs for three years, from 1 January 2026 to 31 December 2028 (granted under the pathway for breakthrough devices; high confidence). Set 2 assumed it ended at end-2027 or end-2028 and put price cuts into 2028 and 2029. The price reset belongs to 2029 and 2030, outside the 18-month window.
  2. The US TOUCH selling price is known only within a wide range. The device cost in the Medicare application is USD 8,985 (the list price of its three components). The chief executive said in March 2026 that US plans were calculated on USD 4,000 to 4,500 a device. Set 2 used about USD 6,500 (management, August 2026, one source). The difference between USD 4,250 and USD 6,500 is worth about two points of FY2027 group growth.
  3. Debt is higher than Set 1 or Set 2 said. At 30 June 2026 (half-year report, via the AlphaSense debt report): bank loans CHF 58.1m (Set 1: 12; Set 2: about 32, which was the end-2025 figure), convertible bond at its carrying value CHF 99.7m, leases CHF 49.5m, earn-out liabilities for KeriMedical and CADskills CHF 20.4m (in neither earlier set), the NeoOrtho buy-out liability CHF 18.4m; total CHF 246m against cash of CHF 25.8m. Net financial debt excluding leases, earn-outs and the NeoOrtho liability was CHF 132m, about 2.4 times trailing core EBITDA (Set 2: CHF 104m, 1.9 times). The company borrowed CHF 26m long term in H1 2026. Known bank facilities are CHF 40m committed plus CHF 50m of "additional headroom"; covenants are not disclosed. The model itself is not affected: it starts from FactSet's total debt of CHF 207m (convertible, bank loans and leases), which is right.
  4. The convertible bond has a holder put as well as an issuer call (medium confidence, one source citing the 2024 placement document): holders may demand repayment on the fifth anniversary, April 2029; the company may call from 2 May 2029 if the share price is at or above CHF 130.94.
  5. The share count is settled. Of 13,632,277 registered shares at end-2025, 1,199,665 are treasury shares the company created and lent to a bank so that convertible-bond investors could hedge; they are excluded from EPS. Shares outstanding were 12.43m at end-2025 and 12.45m at June 2026. The lent shares are, in effect, the shares that conversion would deliver (1,149,665), so FactSet's 14.7m "diluted" count adds the conversion shares twice. Market capitalisation on shares outstanding is about CHF 1,357m (FactSet shows CHF 1,604m). Enterprise value, counting the convertible, bank loans, earn-outs, the NeoOrtho liability and pensions as debt, is about CHF 1,540m before leases and about CHF 1,590m with them (FactSet shows CHF 1,721m).
  6. The tax leakage is structural, not a fixed sum. In the FY2025 reconciliation the Swiss rate (13.04%, rising to about 14.5% in Basel from 2026) gave a theoretical charge of CHF 1.7m; higher foreign rates added CHF 0.8m; and tax effects for which no deferred tax asset is recognised added CHF 5.1m, mainly on profit booked when goods are sold to the group's own foreign subsidiaries. Unrecognised tax losses are CHF 22.5m; there are no advance pricing agreements.
  7. Sector news since Set 1. Medacta issued a profit warning on 5 November 2026 (constant-currency growth of about 9% against a 10% to 14% guide; margin-expansion target dropped; cause: US insurance pre-authorisation denials and higher patient deductibles). Treace's Q3 2026 revenue fell 4%; Globus, Stryker and Smith & Nephew cut 2026 guidance. Against that, Paragon 28 grew 18% and Bonesupport's US sales grew 49% in Q3 2026 after a Q2 pause.
  8. No rival US thumb-base prosthesis is in sight, and the pass-through pathway TOUCH used closed to new applications after 1 October 2026, so a later rival faces a harder test. In France prostheses were 75% of thumb-base operations by 2024. The clinical counter-evidence is also real: the UK Hand Registry finds faster recovery in the first six months but no lasting advantage over trapeziectomy, and revision risk is higher.

  1. The forecast inputs I change, and what they do (details in inputs-changed.md)

Net financial items -7.0 -11.6 -12.1 -12.7 -12.4 Bank debt is CHF 58m, not 32m, and earn-outs add to it (was -6.0, -11.0, -11.3, -11.8, -11.5)

Revenue for FY2026 and FY2027, the EBITDA margin in every year, depreciation, capital spending, working capital and the three half-year inputs are unchanged.

My own arithmetic of the effect, which the Financial Analyst's model will replace with the figures of record (EPS on the model's basis, before minorities):

FY2026 FY2027 FY2028 FY2029 FY2030
EPS, model update 1 1.37 1.84 2.56 3.39 4.31
EPS on the changed inputs (about) 1.24 1.63 2.37 3.20 4.10
Against the two brokers (1.55, 2.50, 3.79; one broker 5.60) -20% -35% -37% -43%

Net financial debt of about CHF 165m at end-2027 implies bank borrowing of about CHF 80m to 85m, close to the CHF 90m of facilities the company has described. The plan is fundable, but without spare room.

  1. The cross-check gaps flagged in the model summary, explained

The model's free cash flow is net income plus depreciation, less capital spending, less the rise in working capital, less lease payments. Set 2's own figure is a cash figure. The gaps (Set 2 against the model: -3.9 against -12.9 in FY2027; 8.2 against 2.9 in FY2028; 19.5 against 14.0 in FY2029) have four causes:

  1. Non-cash and double-counted financing items, about CHF 5.5m to 6m a year. The model deducts all net financial items. Set 2's cash figure left out the convertible's non-cash accretion (about CHF 3.5m) and the small non-cash currency charge (CHF 1m), and it counted lease interest (about CHF 1.2m) once, inside lease payments, where the model counts it twice (in net financial items and again in the lease-payment input). The lease-interest part is an input error, and I correct it by making the lease-payment input principal only. The accretion and currency parts are not input errors; the model's figure is simply stricter than cash by about CHF 4.5m a year, and the Consumer should say so when quoting it.
  2. The US tariff refund, CHF 4.4m, in FY2027 only. Set 2 added its collection on top of the working-capital ratio. The model has no line for it, and management expected to collect it in H2 2026. I change nothing; whichever year it arrives, the model's cash flow for that year is understated by up to CHF 4.4m, once.
  3. Non-core cash costs (about CHF 0.5m to 1m a year; CHF 2.5m in FY2026), which Set 2 deducted and the model does not. This is why Set 2's FY2026 figure (-18.8) is below the model's (-17.6), together with the non-cash currency gain inside FY2026 net income.
  4. Minorities explain the EPS gap (Set 2's EPS is CHF 0.05 to 0.09 below the model's), as the summary already notes; they are not modelled by Richard's ruling.

None of the gaps shows an error in revenue, margin, depreciation, capital spending or working capital.

The third set: the predictability of the financial forecasts for the next 18 months

Appendix Forecast inputs the Investment Analyst changed before the memo

Changes to the forecast inputs: Medartis Holding AG (MED-CH), as at 18-Nov-26

Money in millions of CHF, except per-share figures; costs and outflows negative. Only the lines below change; every other line of the second set's forecast inputs, including all of Part 3, stands as written.

Part 1. Profit and loss inputs, one column for each of the five fiscal years

Input FY2026 (year to 31-Dec-26) FY2027 (year to 31-Dec-27) FY2028 (year to 31-Dec-28) FY2029 (year to 31-Dec-29) FY2030 (year to 31-Dec-30)
Revenue growth (%) 25.7 16.5 15.2 12.3 11.3
Net financial items -7.0 -11.6 -12.1 -12.7 -12.4
Tax rate (%) 42.0 35.0 30.0 26.0 24.0

Revenue growth (%): TOUCH's US Medicare pass-through payment runs to 31 December 2028, so the US price reset that the second set placed in 2028 and 2029 (5% in each) moves to 2029 and 2030 (10%, then 5%), which lifts FY2028 growth from 14.5% and lowers FY2029 and FY2030 growth from 13.0% and 12.0%, while FY2026 and FY2027 are unchanged.

Net financial items: Bank loans were CHF 58.1m at 30 June 2026 and not the CHF 32m the second set assumed, the financing cost before currency was already about CHF 4.9m in H1 2026, and CHF 20.4m of earn-outs will add to borrowing, so the charge is about CHF 0.6m to 1.0m a year higher than the second set's -6.0, -11.0, -11.3, -11.8 and -11.5.

Tax rate (%): The core tax rate in H1 2026 was still about 46% (CHF 5.7m on core pre-tax profit of CHF 12.4m) at twice FY2025's rate of profit, which shows that the leakage (tax on profit booked on sales to the group's own subsidiaries, and unrecognised losses) grows with the business and is not a fixed sum, so the rate falls more slowly than the second set's 38%, 28%, 25%, 23% and 22%.

Part 2. Cash flow and balance sheet inputs, one column for each of the five fiscal years

Input FY2026 (year to 31-Dec-26) FY2027 (year to 31-Dec-27) FY2028 (year to 31-Dec-28) FY2029 (year to 31-Dec-29) FY2030 (year to 31-Dec-30)
Lease payments -7.8 -9.6 -10.6 -11.1 -11.6
Acquisitions less disposals -19.0 -10.0 -9.0 -22.0 -3.0

Lease payments: The model's free cash flow starts from net income, which already bears lease interest inside net financial items, so this line should carry lease principal only (CHF 3.7m was repaid in H1 2026), which removes a double count of about CHF 1.2m to 1.4m a year in the second set's -10.0, -11.0, -12.0, -12.5 and -13.0 and brings FY2026 into line with the H1 run-rate.

Acquisitions less disposals: The balance sheet at 30 June 2026 carries CHF 20.4m of earn-out liabilities for KeriMedical and CADskills that the second set's path (-19.0, -3.0, -2.0, -20.0, -3.0) did not include, which I spread over FY2027 and FY2028 because their timing is not disclosed, and the NeoOrtho buy-out liability (CHF 18.4m at June 2026, up to CHF 24.8m undiscounted) is accreting towards its FY2029 payment.

Co-movement of MED-CH with its dashboard peers (computed 2026-11-17 from the master dashboard's daily prices)

What this is: daily close-to-close returns over rolling windows of 126 trading days (about six months). Correlation: how closely the daily moves line up (1 = always together, 0 = unrelated). Beta: how far the stock moves for a 1% move in the basket. R-squared: the share of the stock's daily price variance the peer basket explains; the remainder is the stock's own (idiosyncratic) movement. Basket: the peers below, equal-weighted. Prices only; the multiple's own history is in the financials block.

Appendix Research requests the Investment Analyst raised for the memo

Research requests for the next set

Company: Medartis Holding AG (MED-CH), the Basel-based maker of titanium implants, instruments and joint prostheses for hand, wrist, elbow, shoulder, foot and ankle and cranio-maxillofacial surgery, run as three brands (Medartis premium fixation; KeriMedical, maker of the TOUCH thumb-base prosthesis; NeoOrtho, a 51%-owned Brazilian value-implant maker) plus CADskills patient-specific implants; it reports Upper Extremities, Lower Extremities and CMF & Others product groups and four regions (EMEA, North America, APAC, LATAM), semi-annually, on a "core" basis.

Request: OWN-1

Question: What have Medartis Holding AG (MED-CH) and its finance director Peter Hackel said about the group's income tax rate for 2026 and later years, and what do the brokers covering Medartis assume in their published forecasts? Specifically: (a) the exact words used in the half-year 2026 report and on the results call of 18 August 2026 about the tax rate expected for the second half of 2026 and for full-year 2026, and on which basis (IFRS or core), kept clearly apart from statements about the roughly 12.5% tariff rate on US imports and the CHF 4.4m US tariff refund; (b) how the H1 2026 core income tax expense of CHF 5.7m against core net profit of CHF 6.8m (company, 18 August 2026) and the FY2025 core income tax expense of CHF 5.2m against core net profit of CHF 6.1m (company, 17 March 2026) reconcile with management's stated long-term tax rate of 18% to 19%, including the tax-reconciliation items in the 2025 annual report (unrecognised temporary differences on intercompany profits, unrecognised tax losses of CHF 22.5m, foreign tax-rate differences) and any statement on when and why the rate should fall; (c) for each covering broker (UBS, Stifel, Zuercher Kantonalbank (ZKB), Octavian, Research Partners and any other), the published forecasts for FY2026, FY2027 and FY2028 of revenue, EBITDA, depreciation and amortisation, EBIT, financial result, tax rate, net income, earnings per share and the share count used, whether these are on the company's core basis or another basis, and the second-half 2026 figures they imply (FactSet at 14 November 2026 shows UBS at EPS of CHF 1.59 for FY2026 and CHF 2.50 for FY2027, and Stifel at CHF 1.41 and CHF 2.50); (d) which broker moved its rating on Medartis off Buy between August and November 2026, on what date and for what stated reason; and (e) how each broker's estimates and target price changed after the FY2025 results of 17 March 2026, the half-year results of 18 August 2026 and the company's release of 2 November 2026.

Request: OWN-2

Question: What are the realised US selling price, revenue and procedure run-rate of the TOUCH thumb carpometacarpal prosthesis sold by Medartis Holding AG (MED-CH) through its KeriMedical brand, and how fast is Medartis's US business growing without it? Specifically: (a) every company statement on the US price or revenue per TOUCH procedure, with date and exact wording, including any statement in August or November 2026 (the device cost in the Medicare pass-through application is USD 8,985 for the cup, the neck and liner, and the stem; the chief executive referred in March 2026 to US calculations based on USD 4,000 to 4,500 per device), and what price the covering brokers assume; (b) US TOUCH procedures by month or quarter in 2026 (the company reported more than 2,000 by October 2026 and projected about 3,000 for the year on 2 November 2026), the number of US hospitals and surgery centres that have approved the device, the number of trained US surgeons who are active users, and any company or broker projection for 2027 and 2028; (c) how Medartis's US sales growth in H1 2026 (27.4% at constant currency, to CHF 30.3m, company) divides between TOUCH and the plating and other fixation products, and any evidence on US TOUCH accounts adopting APTUS plates and screws; (d) the commission rates and support costs of Medartis's US distribution model and any statement on the profitability of its US business; (e) who leads Medartis's US business as of November 2026: whether David Thoni, appointed US President in January 2025, remains in post, whether the finance director Peter Hackel holds the role ad interim, as one reading of the half-year 2026 results presentation suggests, and any announcement of a departure, a search or a successor; and (f) the Medicare procedure code and outpatient payment group that apply to the TOUCH procedure, the facility payment in 2026 and 2027 in hospital outpatient departments and in ambulatory surgery centres, and how Medicare is expected to set the payment once pass-through status ends on 31 December 2028.

Request: OWN-3

Question: How have the share price and the valuation multiple of Medartis Holding AG (MED-CH) behaved around its own results and guidance events and through its growth cycles, and what do comparable specialist medical-device companies show about multiples at peak growth and at the first slowdown? Specifically: (a) the share-price reaction on the day and over the following month for each Medartis results day and trading statement since its 2018 listing, including the 2019 profit warning, the 2022 exit from China, the FY2024 guidance cut, the FY2025 results of 17 March 2026 (sales ahead of expectations, net profit below), the half-year results of 18 August 2026 and the release of 2 November 2026, with the reasons commentators gave; (b) the range of Medartis's forward price-to-earnings, enterprise-value-to-sales and enterprise-value-to-EBITDA multiples over its listed life, the multiple at each growth peak and each trough, and what analysts regard as a fair multiple for the company and why; (c) the same evidence for Medacta (around its 2026 slowdown and its profit warning of 5 November 2026), Bonesupport (around its 2026 US pause and recovery), Straumann, Paragon 28 before its 2025 acquisition by Zimmer Biomet, Treace Medical Concepts, and Tornier and Wright Medical, with how far and how fast each multiple fell when growth first slowed and what it later recovered to; (d) the takeover multiples paid for extremities and specialist orthopaedic companies, including Wright Medical, Paragon 28 and ZimVie; (e) Medartis's shareholder register and trading: the holding and recent share sales of the founder Thomas Straumann, other holders above 3%, the free float, daily liquidity, any short interest, and how the 3.0% convertible bond due 2031 (conversion price CHF 100.725) trades and is hedged; and (f) any published investor commentary on Medartis, including fund letters, investor-conference summaries and social-media discussion, and what it says the debate about the company is.
